# Walkthrough: POST /inventory/{skuId}/purchase (purchase inventory)
- Date: 2026-09-29
- Covers: decc7e0..69b82b2 (plus uncommitted: yes, Robert's comment rewording in InventoryQuantity.kt, InventoryRepository.kt and SkuNotFoundException.kt, and note / interview-prep additions from this session)
- Files: InventoryController.kt, InventoryService.kt, PurchaseResult.kt, InventoryRepository.kt,
  InsufficientInventoryException.kt, InventoryExceptionHandler.kt, InventoryConcurrencyTest.kt
  (application.yml only lost a trailing space; the controller, service and repository tests were summarized)

## Concepts covered
- Request pipeline order: routing → `@RequestBody` (Content-Type, then Jackson) → `@Valid` (`@Min`) → handler → DB. `@Valid` only runs Bean Validation; the other two checks belong to `@RequestBody`.
- A path variable matches one segment: `/{skuId}` never matches `widget/purchase`; `POST /inventory/purchase` is a receive for SKU `purchase`.
- The service takes `String` + `Int`, not the web DTO, so non-HTTP callers don't need a fake request body.
- `require` in the service stops negative purchases, which the DB `CHECK` can't catch (stock goes up).
- `when` with a subject (like `switch`: `is` for a type, equality for a `data object`) vs. without (if/else-if, first true wins, `else` required).
- `when (val x = ...)`: one call, name scoped to the `when`. `return when` works because `when` is an expression; `throw` branches are `Nothing`.
- Smart casts: `is Purchased` unlocks `.item`; `!= null` turns `Int?` into `Int`.
- Sealed interface: closed set of cases, exhaustive `when` with no `else`, so a new case breaks the build where it must be handled.
- `data class` carries data (new instance each time); `data object` is a singleton with a readable `toString`. Nested cases are `PurchaseResult.X`.
- Repository reports facts (`PurchaseResult`), service throws domain exceptions, handler maps to HTTP.
- Data-modifying CTE: the stock check is in the `UPDATE`'s `WHERE`, so check and write are atomic. Zero rows in `purchased` → scalar subquery gives `NULL`; `EXISTS` separates the two failures.
- One snapshot per statement: only `RETURNING` shows the new quantity.

### Claude

The walkthrough gate passes. The only `src/` commits after the purchase walkthrough are the comment rewordings it already covered. I've also created the branch `test/oversell-hardening` from an up-to-date `main`.

## 1. Contract check

This feature adds tests, not endpoints, so there's no new behavior to pin down. The contract-validation test does check all four operations in `inventory.yaml`:

- [ ] `GET /inventory` returns 200 `application/json` with an array of `InventoryItem`
- [ ] `GET /inventory/{skuId}` returns 200 `application/json` `InventoryItem`, or 404 `text/plain` `Error` (a string)
- [ ] `POST /inventory/{skuId}` returns 200 `application/json` `InventoryItem`, or 400 `text/plain`
- [ ] `POST /inventory/{skuId}/purchase` returns 200 `application/json` `InventoryItem`, or 400 or 404 `text/plain`
- [ ] Requests use the `InventoryQuantity` schema (`quantity` is a required integer, `minimum: 1`). `InventoryItem.quantity` has `minimum: 0`.

**Ambiguity:** `InventoryItem` marks no properties as `required` and doesn't forbid extra fields. A plain schema validator would accept `{}`. I suggest the test also asserts that both fields are present and that nothing extra comes back. That's stricter than the YAML, but it matches what the endpoints actually promise.

## 2. Plan

Almost everything here is test code, so the `src/main/` rule doesn't really come into play. Every piece lands as its own small commit.

| # | Part | Test files | How it works |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Shared helper | `support/Race.kt` | Move `race()` out of `InventoryConcurrencyTest` so every part below can use it. |
| a | Negative control | `NaiveInventoryRepository.kt` + `NegativeControlTest.kt` | A naive purchase in `src/test`: read, check in Kotlin, then write the absolute value. It runs through the same race, and the test asserts that the same invariant check **catches** the oversell. A `CyclicBarrier` between the read and the write makes the lost update happen on every run instead of only sometimes. |
| e | Contract validation | `InventoryContractTest.kt` | Checks every status code of every operation against `inventory.yaml` through MockMvc. |
| d | Property-based | `InventoryModelPropertyTest.kt` | Random sequences of receive and purchase calls on a few SKUs run against the real repository and a simple in-memory model at the same time. After every step, the result and the stored quantity must match the model. |
| b | Multi-instance | `MultiInstanceRaceTest.kt` | Two full Spring apps start on random ports against the same Testcontainers Postgres. Buyers are split across both apps, and the invariants must hold. |
| c | HTTP load | `InventoryLoadTest.kt` + a Gradle `loadTest` task | Sustained concurrent purchases on a few popular SKUs, then check the final quantities with `GET /inventory`. |
| — | Docs | README, TODO | Make the README's testing claims match what exists (gap audit items 2 and 3). |

**New concepts** (also the walkthrough agenda):
- a `CyclicBarrier` to force a specific interleaving of threads
- sharing test helpers as top-level functions
- OpenAPI response validation
- Kotest `Arb` generators and `checkAll`, with your first contact with coroutines (`suspend`, `runBlocking`)
- model-based testing
- `SpringApplicationBuilder` to run two app contexts
- `java.net.http.HttpClient` and virtual threads
- JUnit `@Tag` and a custom Gradle `Test` task

**My recommendations for the rest:**
- **Order:** 0 → a → e → d → b → c → docs. It goes from no new dependencies to the heaviest setup.
- **`@RepeatedTest`:** keep it at 5 in the normal build and let the `loadTest` task provide the volume.

I need your call on four decisions:

// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/main/inventory/PurchaseResult.kt.md

package com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory

// Why: We used the sealed interface because we have 3 possible outcomes we need to account for. Not just a success or null so returning InventoryItem? doesn't help us between when we need to throw 404 or 400.

/** PurchaseResult is returned by the repository and translated by the service. */
sealed interface PurchaseResult {
    
    /** The SKU exists and had enough stock. Item holds the remaining stock. */
    data class Purchased(val item: InventoryItem) : PurchaseResult
    
    /** There's no row for that SKU id. */
    data object SkuNotFound : PurchaseResult
    
    /** The row exists but has less than the requested quantity. */
    data object InsufficientStock : PurchaseResult
    
}// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/main/inventory/InventoryItem.kt.md

{
  "file_path": "C:\\Users\\rober\\Documents\\Projects\\Nuuly_Inventory_Assessment\\src\\test\\kotlin\\com\\robertwoolbright\\inventory\\support\\Race.kt",
  "content": "// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/support/Race.kt.md\npackage com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support\n\nimport java.util.concurrent.Callable\nimport java.util.concurrent.CountDownLatch\nimport java.util.concurrent.Executors\nimport java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit\n\n// Why: a start gate. Every thread first checks in on `ready`, then waits on `start`; releasing\n// `start` once makes them all hit the database together instead of one after another.\n/**\n * Runs [action] on [threads] threads that all start at the same moment, and returns each thread's\n * outcome in thread-index order. An exception inside [action] becomes a failed [Result] instead of\n * stopping the race. Shared by every race test in the oversell test plan.\n */\nfun <T> race(threads: Int, action: (Int) -> T): List<Result<T>> {\n    val pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threads)\n    val ready = CountDownLatch(threads)\n    val start = CountDownLatch(1)\n    try {\n        val futures = (0 until threads).map { i ->\n            pool.submit(Callable {\n                ready.countDown()\n                start.await()\n                action(i)\n            })\n        }\n        ready.await()\n        start.countDown()\n        return futures.map { runCatching { it.get(30, TimeUnit.SECONDS) } }\n    } finally {\n        pool.shutdownNow()\n    }\n}\n"
}
```

  "content": "// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/inventory/OversellAssertions.kt.md\npackage com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory\n\nimport kotlin.test.assertEquals\nimport kotlin.test.assertTrue\n\n// Why: one shared check, so the negative control proves that the exact assertions guarding the\n// real repository can detect an oversell, not a separate check written to fail.\n/**\n * The oversell invariant for a race of single-unit purchases: [outcomes] holds one result per buyer,\n * the SKU started with [stock] units and ended at [finalQuantity]. With at least as many buyers as\n * units, exactly [stock] purchases succeed, each unit is sold exactly once, the rest are refused,\n * and the SKU ends at 0.\n */\nfun assertNoOversell(stock: Int, outcomes: List<PurchaseResult>, finalQuantity: Int?) {\n    require(outcomes.size >= stock) { \"needs at least $stock buyers, got ${outcomes.size}\" }\n    assertTrue(outcomes.none { it == PurchaseResult.SkuNotFound }, \"an existing SKU was reported missing\")\n\n    val remaining = outcomes.filterIsInstance<PurchaseResult.Purchased>().map { it.item.quantity }.sorted()\n    assertTrue(remaining.size <= stock, \"oversold: ${remaining.size} purchases succeeded for a stock of $stock\")\n    assertEquals((0 until stock).toList(), remaining, \"each unit must be sold exactly once\")\n    assertEquals(outcomes.size - stock, outcomes.count { it == PurchaseResult.InsufficientStock }, \"refused purchases\")\n    assertEquals(0, finalQuantity, \"final quantity\")\n}\n"
}

  "content": "// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/inventory/NegativeControlTest.kt.md\npackage com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory\n\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.TestcontainersConfiguration\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.race\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.BeforeEach\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.Test\nimport org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Autowired\nimport org.springframework.boot.test.context.SpringBootTest\nimport org.springframework.context.annotation.Import\nimport org.springframework.jdbc.core.simple.JdbcClient\nimport java.util.concurrent.CyclicBarrier\nimport java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit\nimport kotlin.test.assertFailsWith\nimport kotlin.test.assertTrue\n\n/**\n * Oversell test plan, negative control: proves the race check in [assertNoOversell] can fail. A\n * naive read-then-write purchase ([NaiveInventoryRepository], test-only) passes the check when\n * buyers take turns, and fails it when they race.\n */\n@SpringBootTest\n@Import(TestcontainersConfiguration::class)\nclass NegativeControlTest @Autowired constructor(\n    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,\n) {\n\n    private val stock = 10\n    private val buyers = 12\n\n    /** Every test starts with one SKU, `widget`, holding [stock] units. */\n    @BeforeEach\n    fun seedWidget() {\n        jdbcClient.sql(\"DELETE FROM inventory\").update()\n        jdbcClient.sql(\"INSERT INTO inventory (sku_id, quantity) VALUES ('widget', ?)\").param(stock).update()\n    }\n\n    private fun quantityOf(skuId: String): Int? =\n        jdbcClient.sql(\"SELECT quantity FROM inventory WHERE sku_id = ?\")\n            .param(skuId)\n            .query { rs, _ -> rs.getInt(\"quantity\") }\n            .list()\n            .singleOrNull()\n\n    /** Without concurrency the naive code is correct, so a failure below comes from the race alone. */\n    @Test\n    fun `the naive purchase passes the oversell check when buyers take turns`() {\n        val naive = NaiveInventoryRepository(jdbcClient) {}\n\n        val outcomes = (1..buyers).map { naive.removeStock(\"widget\", 1) }\n\n        assertNoOversell(stock, outcomes, quantityOf(\"widget\"))\n    }\n\n    // Why: a real race only sometimes interleaves badly. The barrier holds every buyer between its\n    // read and its write until all of them have read, so the lost update happens on every run.\n    /**\n     * With every buyer reading stock 10 before anyone writes, all of them \"succeed\" and all write 9.\n     * No error is raised and the database CHECK never fires, yet the oversell check fails.\n     */\n    @Test\n    fun `the oversell check catches a naive purchase under a race`() {\n        val barrier = CyclicBarrier(buyers)\n        val naive = NaiveInventoryRepository(jdbcClient) { barrier.await(10, TimeUnit.SECONDS) }\n\n        val results = race(buyers) { naive.removeStock(\"widget\", 1) }\n\n        val failures = results.mapNotNull { it.exceptionOrNull() }\n        assertTrue(failures.isEmpty(), \"the naive purchases raised errors: $failures\")\n        val outcomes = results.map { it.getOrThrow() }\n        val caught = assertFailsWith<AssertionError> {\n            assertNoOversell(stock, outcomes, quantityOf(\"widget\"))\n        }\n        assertTrue(caught.message.orEmpty().startsWith(\"oversold\"), \"expected an oversell, got: ${caught.message}\")\n    }\n}\n"
}

# Study notes

One notes file per source file. The code stays clean, with short KDoc and one-line
`// Why:` / `// Contract:` comments. The teaching lives here.

## Where each file's notes live

The notes tree mirrors the source tree, with the long package prefix dropped:

| Source | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| `src/main/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/<path>.kt` | `docs/learning/notes/main/<path>.kt.md` |
| `src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/<path>.kt` | `docs/learning/notes/test/<path>.kt.md` |
| `src/main/resources/<path>` (`application*.yml`, `db/migration/*.sql`) | `docs/learning/notes/main/resources/<path>.md` |
| `<root file>` (`*.gradle.kts`, `compose.yaml`) | `docs/learning/notes/<root file>.md` |

## Finding the notes from the code

Every source file's first line points to its notes, written in that file's comment syntax:

```kotlin
// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/main/InventoryApplication.kt.md
```

In VS Code, select the path and press **Ctrl+P** to open it. (The editor doesn't turn relative
paths in comments into links. IntelliJ usually does.) `check_notes.py` checks that the header is
there and correct, so a moved or renamed file can't keep pointing to the wrong note.

## Format

```markdown
# <FileName>

> Source: [path](link) · Last synced with code: YYYY-MM-DD

## Role
Where this file sits (controller → service → repository) and why it exists.

## Walkthrough
### <short heading per chunk>
​```kotlin
<the chunk, quoted from the source>
​```
**Kotlin:** language features, with the Java equivalent when that helps.
**Spring:** framework behavior the code doesn't show.
**Why:** the decision, and the alternative we rejected.
**Contract:** the part of inventory.yaml this satisfies.

## Check your understanding
- Questions to answer without looking back.
```

Code is **quoted, not referenced by line number**, so the notes survive edits elsewhere in
the file, and a stale quote is easy to spot (`doc-reviewer` checks for them). Quotes are
compared ignoring indentation, and a line containing only `...` marks lines left out on purpose.

## Keeping them in sync

- `/sync-docs` updates the notes and the `Study notes:` header lines (plus the concepts index, README and TODO) for whatever changed.

<details><summary>Tool result</summary>

````
40	- A row the test inserts before the race (`widget` with 10) is uncommitted, so the workers can't
41	  see it. Worse, each worker's upsert would wait on that row's lock, which the test thread holds
42	  until the rollback, so the race would hang until the timeout.
43	- The workers' rows are committed for real, so the rollback wouldn't clean them up, and they'd
44	  leak into the next test.
45	
46	So this class runs with no test transaction. Every call commits, exactly like in production,
47	and the `@BeforeEach` `DELETE` does the cleanup (the same pattern as
48	[InventoryControllerTest.kt](InventoryControllerTest.kt.md)).
49	
50	**Spring: context caching.** These annotations differ from `InventoryControllerTest` (there's no
51	`@AutoConfigureMockMvc`), so Spring caches a separate context for this class, with its own
52	Postgres container. That costs a few seconds of startup, and in return the class doesn't depend on
53	MockMvc, which isn't meant to be called from many threads.
54	
55	### The start gate
56	
57	```kotlin
58	// Why: a start gate. Every thread first checks in on `ready`, then waits on `start`; releasing
59	// `start` once makes them all hit the database together instead of one after another.
60	private fun <T> race(threads: Int, action: (Int) -> T): List<Result<T>> {
61	    val pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threads)
62	    val ready = CountDownLatch(threads)
63	    val start = CountDownLatch(1)
64	    try {
65	        val futures = (0 until threads).map { i ->
66	            pool.submit(Callable {
67	                ready.countDown()
68	                start.await()
69	                action(i)
70	            })
71	        }
72	        ready.await()
73	        start.countDown()
74	        return futures.map { runCatching { it.get(30, TimeUnit.SECONDS) } }
75	    } finally {
76	        pool.shutdownNow()
77	    }
78	}
79	```
80	
81	**Why a gate at all:** starting 50 threads one by one in a loop spreads them out in time. The
82	first threads may finish before the last ones start, and then there's no race to test. The gate
83	makes them all wait at the same line and releases them together.
84	
85	**Java concurrency: `CountDownLatch`.** A counter threads can wait on. `countDown()` decrements
86	it; `await()` blocks until it reaches zero. There are two latches here:
87	
88	1. `ready` starts at `threads`. Each worker counts it down as it arrives, and the test thread's
89	   `ready.await()` returns once all of them are waiting.
90	2. `start` starts at 1. Every worker blocks on `start.await()`. The test's single
91	   `start.countDown()` releases all of them at once.
92	
93	**Java concurrency: thread pool and futures.** `Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threads)` makes one
94	thread per worker, so all of them can wait at the gate together (a smaller pool would deadlock:
95	the queued tasks could never arrive at `ready`). `submit` returns a `Future`; `get(30, SECONDS)`
96	waits for its result and rethrows anything the task threw, wrapped in an `ExecutionException`.
97	The timeout makes a hung test fail instead of running forever. `shutdownNow()` in `finally`
98	stops the threads even when something failed.
99	
100	**Kotlin: a generic function.** `fun <T> race(..., action: (Int) -> T): List<Result<T>>` works for
101	any result type. `T` is whatever `action` returns: `InventoryItem?` for `addStock`,
102	`PurchaseResult` for `removeStock`, a `Pair` or an `Int` in the purchase races below. Java writes it the same way: `<T> List<Result<T>> race(...)`.
103	
104	**Kotlin: function types.** `action: (Int) -> T` is a parameter that holds a function taking an
105	`Int` (the thread's index) and returning a `T`. Java would use `IntFunction<T>` or
106	`Function<Integer, T>`. The call sites pass a trailing lambda: `race(threads) { i -> ... }`.
107	
108	**Kotlin: `Callable { ... }`.** `submit` is overloaded for `Runnable` (no result) and `Callable`
109	(returns a result). Writing `Callable { ... }` picks the one that returns a value.
... [truncated 13732 chars; see raw log]
````

- Why would these tests be unreliable under `@JdbcTest`? Give both reasons.
- What happens to the race if you delete the `start` latch and keep only `ready`? And if you
  delete both?
- Why must the thread pool have `threads` threads, and not, say, 10?

  100 units to sell?

### The start gate


```kotlin
// Why: a start gate. Every thread first checks in on `ready`, then waits on `start`; releasing
// `start` once makes them all hit the database together instead of one after another.
fun <T> race(threads: Int, action: (Int) -> T): List<Result<T>> {
    val pool = Executors.newFixedThreadPool(threads)
````

  "content": "# OversellAssertions.kt\n\n> Source: [src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/OversellAssertions.kt](../../../../../src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/OversellAssertions.kt) · Last synced with code: 2026-09-30\n\n## Role\n\nThe oversell invariant, written once as a function. After a race of single-unit purchases, it\nchecks that nothing was oversold. Two tests call it:\n\n- [InventoryConcurrencyTest.kt](InventoryConcurrencyTest.kt.md) runs it against the real\n  `InventoryRepository.removeStock` and expects it to **pass**.\n- [NegativeControlTest.kt](NegativeControlTest.kt.md) runs it against a naive read-then-write\n  purchase and expects it to **fail**.\n\n## Walkthrough\n\n### Why the check is shared\n\n```kotlin\n// Why: one shared check, so the negative control proves that the exact assertions guarding the\n// real repository can detect an oversell, not a separate check written to fail.\n```\n\n**Why:** a negative control is only worth something if it tests the *same* check. If the negative\ncontrol had its own assertions, it would only prove that *those* can fail. The real race test\ncould still have a weak check (say, only \"final quantity >= 0\", which the naive code passes).\nSharing one function removes that gap.\n\n**Kotlin: a top-level function in the test package.** Like `race` in\n[Race.kt](../support/Race.kt.md), it lives directly in the file. It sits in the `inventory`\npackage, next to the tests, because it works on `PurchaseResult`.\n\n### The precondition\n\n```kotlin\nfun assertNoOversell(stock: Int, outcomes: List<PurchaseResult>, finalQuantity: Int?) {\n    require(outcomes.size >= stock) { \"needs at least $stock buyers, got ${outcomes.size}\" }\n    assertTrue(outcomes.none { it == PurchaseResult.SkuNotFound }, \"an existing SKU was reported missing\")\n```\n\n**Kotlin: `require` vs. an assertion.** `require` throws `IllegalArgumentException`: *the caller*\nused the function wrongly (fewer buyers than units, so \"exactly `stock` succeed\" can't be\nexpected). The `assert...` functions throw `AssertionError`: *the code under test* is wrong.\nKeeping them apart matters in the negative control, which expects an `AssertionError`, so a\nmisconfigured test can't pass by accident. `require` was introduced in\n[InventoryService.kt](../../main/inventory/InventoryService.kt.md).\n\n**Kotlin: `none { }`.** True when no element matches; the opposite of `any { }`.\n\n**Kotlin: comparing to a `data object`.** `it == PurchaseResult.SkuNotFound` works because a\n`data object` is a single instance with a generated `equals`.\n\n### The four checks, in order\n\n```kotlin\nval remaining = outcomes.filterIsInstance<PurchaseResult.Purchased>().map { it.item.quantity }.sorted()\nassertTrue(remaining.size <= stock, \"oversold: ${remaining.size} purchases succeeded for a stock of $stock\")\nassertEquals((0 until stock).toList(), remaining, \"each unit must be sold exactly once\")\nassertEquals(outcomes.size - stock, outcomes.count { it == PurchaseResult.InsufficientStock }, \"refused purchases\")\nassertEquals(0, finalQuantity, \"final quantity\")\n```\n\nEach check catches a different bug:\n\n1. **Oversold:** more purchases succeeded than there were units. This is the invariant itself,\n   so it goes first, and its message starts with `oversold` (the negative control checks for it).\n2. **Each unit sold exactly once:** every successful buyer was told a *different* remaining\n   quantity, `0, 1, ..., stock - 1`. A lost update shows up as a repeated value.\n3. **Refused purchases:** everyone who didn't get a unit got `InsufficientStock`.\n4. **Final quantity 0:** not negative, and not above 0 (a buyer refused while stock remained).\n\n**Kotlin: `filterIsInstance<T>()`.** Keeps only the elements that are a `T`, *and* gives back

Robert\nwrote it (decided 2026-09-30).\n\n## Walkthrough\n\n### Setup\n\n```kotlin\n@SpringBootTest\n@Import(TestcontainersConfiguration::class)\nclass NegativeControlTest @Autowired constructor(\n    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,\n) {\n\n    private val stock = 10\n    private val buyers = 12\n```\n\n**Spring:** a full context with no test transaction, for the same reason as\n`InventoryConcurrencyTest` (see its notes on per-thread transactions). The naive repository is not\na Spring bean. The test builds it with `NaiveInventoryRepository(jdbcClient) { ... }`, so each test\npicks its own pause.\n\n**Why 12 buyers for 10 units:** it's the scenario from the purchase walkthrough's last question.\nThere are more buyers than units, so a correct purchase must refuse two of them.\n\n### Control 1: the naive code works without a race\n\n```kotlin\n/** Without concurrency the naive code is correct, so a failure below comes from the race alone. */\n@Test\nfun `the naive purchase passes the oversell check when buyers take turns`() {\n    val naive = NaiveInventoryRepository(jdbcClient) {}\n\n    val outcomes = (1..buyers).map { naive.removeStock(\"widget\", 1) }\n\n    assertNoOversell(stock, outcomes, quantityOf(\"widget\"))\n}\n```\n\n**Why:** without this test, the next one could \"catch\" a naive repository that is simply buggy\n(say, it subtracts 2). Passing here pins the blame for the failure below on concurrency alone.\n\n**Kotlin: an empty lambda.** `{}` is a function that takes nothing and does nothing: no pause\nbetween the read and the write. It's written after the parentheses, as a trailing lambda.\n\n**Kotlin: `(1..buyers).map { ... }`.** Runs the purchase twelve times, one after another on the\ntest thread, and collects the results in a list.\n\n### Control 2: the check catches the race\n\n```kotlin\n// Why: a real race only sometimes interleaves badly. The barrier holds every buyer between its\n// read and its write until all of them have read, so the lost update happens on every run.\n...\n@Test\nfun `the oversell check catches a naive purchase under a race`() {\n    val barrier = CyclicBarrier(buyers)\n    val naive = NaiveInventoryRepository(jdbcClient) { barrier.await(10, TimeUnit.SECONDS) }\n\n    val results = race(buyers) { naive.removeStock(\"widget\", 1) }\n\n    val failures = results.mapNotNull { it.exceptionOrNull() }\n    assertTrue(failures.isEmpty(), \"the naive purchases raised errors: $failures\")\n    val outcomes = results.map { it.getOrThrow() }\n    val caught = assertFailsWith<AssertionError> {\n        assertNoOversell(stock, outcomes, quantityOf(\"widget\"))\n    }\n    assertTrue(caught.message.orEmpty().startsWith(\"oversold\"), \"expected an oversell, got: ${caught.message}\")\n}\n```\n\n**Java concurrency: `CyclicBarrier`.** A meeting point for a fixed number of threads. Each thread\ncalls `await()` and blocks until all `buyers` threads have arrived; then all of them continue at\nonce. | `@ParameterizedTest` + `@ValueSource` | One test method run once per supplied value, each reported separately | [InventoryControllerTest.kt](notes/test/inventory/InventoryControllerTest.kt.md) |
| `@RepeatedTest` | Runs a test N times; used for intermittent concurrency bugs | [InventoryConcurrencyTest.kt](notes/test/inventory/InventoryConcurrencyTest.kt.md) |
| Test transactions are per thread | A `@JdbcTest` rollback covers only the test thread; worker threads commit on their own connections | [InventoryConcurrencyTest.kt](notes/test/inventory/InventoryConcurrencyTest.kt.md) |
| Start gate (`CountDownLatch`) | Hold N threads at one line, then release them together so they really overlap | [Race.kt](notes/test/support/Race.kt.md) |
| `CyclicBarrier` | N threads meet at `await()` and continue together; forces a chosen interleaving | [NegativeControlTest.kt](notes/test/inventory/NegativeControlTest.kt.md) |
| Negative control | Run the check against deliberately broken code and assert it fails, proving the check can detect the bug | [NegativeControlTest.kt](notes/test/inventory/NegativeControlTest.kt.md) |
| Request processing order | Routing → `@RequestBody` (Content-Type, then Jackson) → `@Valid` → the handler function; a bad body is a `400` before any database lookup could give a `404` | [InventoryController.kt](notes/main/inventory/InventoryController.kt.md#post-inventoryskuidpurchase) |

## User — 2026-09-30T15:46:58.123Z

This first test negative control test. Lets talk about what it is and what case we are testing

### Claude

A negative control is a **test of your tests**.

## The problem it solves

`InventoryConcurrencyTest` is green: 50 buyers race for 10 units and nothing oversells. But green only means something if the test **could** have gone red. There are two ways it could be green:

1. `removeStock` really is safe under concurrency.
2. The test can't see an oversell. Maybe the race never overlaps, or the assertions are too weak, e.g. only checking "final quantity ≥ 0".

From outside, those two look the same. The negative control rules out #2: feed the test code we **know** is broken and confirm it goes red.

The term comes from lab science. You run the experiment on a sample you know has no effect, to prove your instrument isn't just always reading "positive". Here the instrument is `assertNoOversell`, and the known-bad sample is your naive repository.

## The case: a lost update

The broken code is the naive purchase you described in the walkthrough:

```
read quantity        →  10
check in Kotlin      →  10 >= 1, ok
write absolute value →  SET quantity = 9
```

Each step is fine alone. The bug is the gap between the read and the write. With 12 buyers and 10 units:

| | Buyers 1–12 |
|---|---|
| Read | all read `10` |
| Kotlin check | all pass |
| Write | all write `9` |
| Told | 12 × "Purchased, 9 left" |

Twelve sales from a stock of 10, and the database ends at **9**, not 0. No exception is thrown. `CHECK (quantity >= 0)` never fires, because 9 is a perfectly valid number. That's why this bug is dangerous: the only way to catch it is an assertion that counts what was sold.

## Why two tests

**Test 1, buyers take turns:** the naive code runs 12 times, one after another. It behaves correctly: 10 sold, 2 refused, ends at 0, and the check passes. That proves your naive code isn't simply buggy (subtracting wrong, say). Any failure in test 2 is caused by concurrency and nothing else.

**Test 2, buyers race:** same code, same check, but the buyers run at once. Now the check must fail, and it must fail on the "oversold" assertion specifically, not some other assertion error.

## Why the `CyclicBarrier`

A real race is luck. Buyers usually get between each other's read and write, but not always. If the test relied on luck, it could flake. So the barrier, passed in as your `pauseBeforeWrite`, holds every buyer after its read until all 12 have read. Only then does anyone write. That forces the worst-case interleaving on every run, so the test is deterministic.

That's also why `pauseBeforeWrite` is a constructor parameter. Test 1 passes `{}` (no pause), and test 2 passes the barrier. It's the same repository with a different hook.

## Why it's in `src/test`

It's deliberately wrong code. It exists only to be caught, so it must never ship. Keeping it next to the tests makes it permanent proof, not a one-off experiment someone ran once.

**Check yourself:** if you removed the "each unit sold exactly once" assertion from `assertNoOversell`, would test 2 still catch the naive code? What if you also removed the "oversold" one?

Three small fixes before we move on:

## Step 2: `removeStock`

Signature: `fun removeStock(skuId: String, quantity: Int): PurchaseResult`, with a one-line KDoc.

Inside, in this order:

1. **Read.** Get the current quantity for `skuId` as an `Int?`. It's null when there's no row. The pattern is `findBySkuId` in `InventoryRepository`: `.sql(...)`, `.param(...)`, `.query { rs, _ -> ... }`, `.list().singleOrNull()`. You only need the `quantity` column, so the row mapper can return an `Int` instead of an `InventoryItem`.
2. **Decide.** No row means `PurchaseResult.SkuNotFound`. Too little stock means `PurchaseResult.InsufficientStock`. You wrote this kind of decision in the service with `when`, and it works here too.
3. **Compute** the new quantity in Kotlin.
4. **Pause:** call `pauseBeforeWrite()`. You call a function stored in a property with parentheses, just like `action(i)` in `race`.
5. **Write** the **absolute** value: `UPDATE inventory SET quantity = :quantity WHERE sku_id = :skuId`, finished with `.update()` instead of `.query`.
6. **Return** `PurchaseResult.Purchased(InventoryItem(skuId, newQuantity))`.

**One Kotlin detail for step 2:** after `if (current == null) return ...`, the compiler knows `current` is an `Int` from then on. That's the same smart cast you saw with `!= null` in the service, so the comparison in the next check compiles without `!!`.

### Claude

You're trying to fit the whole purchase into **one chain** (`jdbcClient.sql(...).param(...).update(...).query(...)`). The naive version is really **two separate database calls with Kotlin code between them**, and one chain can't do that.

## Expression body vs. block body

Your `findBySkuId` uses an **expression body**: `fun f(...): T = <one expression>`. That fits because it does one thing, a single chain from `jdbcClient.sql(...)` to `.singleOrNull()`.

`removeStock` does six things in order, so it needs a **block body**. That's curly braces with several statements, like a Java method:

```
fun removeStock(skuId: String, quantity: Int): PurchaseResult {
    statement 1
    statement 2
    ...
    return something
}
```

With `{ }` you need an explicit `return`. With `=` you don't.

## The shape

```
fun removeStock(skuId: String, quantity: Int): PurchaseResult {
    // 1. READ: one complete chain, saved in a val
    val current: Int? = jdbcClient.sql("SELECT quantity FROM ... WHERE sku_id = :skuId")
        .param(...)
        .query { rs, _ -> <read the quantity column as an Int> }
        .list().singleOrNull()

    // 2. DECIDE: plain Kotlin, no database
    if (current == null) return <not found>
    if (<current too small>) return <insufficient>

    // 3. COMPUTE
    val newQuantity = ...

    // 4. PAUSE
    pauseBeforeWrite()

    // 5. WRITE: a second, separate chain
    jdbcClient.sql("UPDATE inventory SET quantity = :quantity WHERE sku_id = :skuId")
        .param(...)
        .param(...)
        .update()

    // 6. RETURN
    return <purchased, with an InventoryItem>
}
```

The comments are only there to show the shape; don't type them.

## What to drop from your current version

- **`sku_exists`, `getObject(... javaObjectType)` and the `when`:** those belong to the real repository's single CTE statement. The naive version doesn't need any of them. The read returns one `Int` or no row at all, and `singleOrNull()` turns "no row" into `null`.
- **`.update("""...""")`:** `.update()` takes no SQL. It **runs** the statement you already gave to `.sql(...)`. The UPDATE SQL goes into its own `jdbcClient.sql(...)` call.
- **The two copied `// Why:` comments:** they explain decisions in the real repository. Only the top `// Why:` belongs here.
- **The missing KDoc on `removeStock`:** it still needs a one-liner.

Fill in the `<...>` parts. Each one is something you've written before. Show me when you're done.

### Claude

Good progress; the shape is right now. Here's what's left, most important first:

**1. Line 15: `= {` is a trap.** With `=`, the braces don't open a function body. They create a **lambda**, so the function would *return a function* instead of running the code. Delete the `=`: `fun removeStock(...): PurchaseResult {`. Block body means no `=`.

**2. Line 17: missing colon.** It should be `val current: Int? = ...`. Same `name: Type` rule as the constructor.

**3. Lines 21–23: the row mapper returns nothing.** A lambda gives back its **last expression**. Yours ends with a `val` declaration, which isn't a value, so the lambda returns `Unit`. Two more problems in there:
- The column is `quantity`. `remaining_quantity` only existed in the real repository's CTE.
- You don't need `getObject(... javaObjectType)`. If the row is missing, the mapper never runs, and `.singleOrNull()` gives `null`. So the lambda body is just one expression reading the `quantity` column as an `Int`. Look at the `rs.get...` call `findBySkuId` uses for quantity.

**4. Line 29: `remainingQuantity` doesn't exist out here.** It was declared inside the lambda, so it disappears when the lambda ends. Compare against `current`. After line 28, `current` is smart-cast to `Int`. Also, `quntity` is a typo.

**5. Line 31: `""` is a `String`.** `newQuantity` is the arithmetic: stock minus what's bought.

**6. Line 38: this one matters most.** You're writing `quantity`, the amount *bought*, into the column. Buy 1 and the stock becomes 1. The absolute write has to be `newQuantity`. That's the line that makes this the lost-update version.

**7. Lines 36 and 41: `.update()` doesn't return the new quantity.** It returns an `Int` counting **how many rows changed** (here, 1). Drop `val final =`, just run the update, and build the `InventoryItem` from `newQuantity`.

**8. Tidy-up:**
- Delete the two copied `// Why:` comments (lines 19 and 24).
- Add a one-line KDoc above `removeStock`.

After these, run `.\gradlew.bat test --tests "*NegativeControlTest"`.

### Claude

Line 21 should be just the value itself:

```kotlin
.query { rs, _ -> rs.getInt("quantity") }
```

**Why no `val`:** a lambda's result is its last expression. The mapper's only job is to *hand back* a value for each row, so the value goes on its own. There's nothing to name and store. Compare `findBySkuId`: its mapper body is just `InventoryItem(...)`, with no `val` in front.

Line 21 currently has two `=` signs. That tries to assign to `quantity`, which is the function's parameter, and parameters are read-only in Kotlin. The compiler will reject that too.

**Where the value ends up:** the lambda returns an `Int` for the row. `.list()` collects them into a `List<Int>`, `.singleOrNull()` turns that into an `Int?`, and it lands in `current` on line 17. So the naming happens on line 17, not inside the lambda.

**One more bug, line 29:** the subtraction is backwards. `quantity - current` is 1 − 10 = −9. You want stock minus purchase: `current - quantity`. As written, the database's `CHECK (quantity >= 0)` would reject the write, so the test would fail for the wrong reason.

And a reminder: `removeStock` still needs its one-line KDoc. Then run the test.

what is your suggesstion for the kdoc?

### Claude

Here are both, for you to type. They're short and say *what's naive*, not just *what it's for*.

**Class (line 9):**

```kotlin
/**
 * A purchase done the naive way: read the stock, check it in Kotlin, then write an absolute value.
 * Correct when buyers take turns; under concurrency, buyers overwrite each other and it oversells.
 */
```

**`removeStock` (line 14):**

```kotlin
/** Sells [quantity] of [skuId] in three separate steps, calling [pauseBeforeWrite] between the read and the write. */
```

**Why these over what's there now:**

- The class KDoc names the **mechanism** (read → check → absolute write) and the **consequence** (oversell). Someone who only reads the KDoc knows why it's broken without opening `NegativeControlTest`. "For our race condition testing" says *who uses it*, and your `// Why:` above it already covers that.
- The method KDoc mentions `pauseBeforeWrite`, because that's the one thing a caller can't guess: *where* the pause happens.
- The square brackets (`[quantity]`, `[skuId]`, `[pauseBeforeWrite]`) are KDoc links, like Javadoc's `{@link}`. The IDE makes them clickable.

The `// Why:` and the class KDoc can't just repeat each other. The `// Why:` explains why the class **exists** (test-only, deliberately unsafe, for the negative control). The KDoc describes **what it does**. With these versions they cover different ground.

Once you've changed them, tell me and I'll update the quote in the study notes to match.

package com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory

import org.springframework.jdbc.core.simple.JdbcClient

// Why: This repository lives only in test because the class is deliberately unsafe. 
// We have it so NegativeControlTest can prove the oversell check catches a lost update.
/**
 * A purchase done the naive way: read the stock, check it in Kotlin, then write an absolute value.
 * Correct when buyers take turns; under concurrency, buyers overwrite each other and it oversells.
 */
class NaiveInventoryRepository(
    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,
    private val pauseBeforeWrite: () -> Unit
) {
    /** Sells [quantity] of [skuId] in three separate steps, calling [pauseBeforeWrite] between the read and the write. */
    fun removeStock(skuId: String, quantity: Int): PurchaseResult  {
        
        val current: Int? = jdbcClient.sql("SELECT quantity FROM inventory WHERE sku_id = :skuId")
        
            .param("skuId", skuId)

## Next: part (e), the contract-validation test

This test sends a request to every response case in `inventory.yaml` through MockMvc and checks the status, content type and body against the YAML. That covers every status code on all four endpoints.

One decision first. `inventory.yaml` defines `InventoryItem` with `skuId` and `quantity`, but doesn't mark either as `required` and doesn't forbid extra fields. A plain validator would therefore accept `{}` or `{"skuId": "x", "quantity": 1, "debug": true}`.

- **Strict (my recommendation):** the test also requires both fields and rejects extra ones. That's stricter than the YAML but matches what the endpoints actually return. I'd record it in the README's contract-gaps section.
- **Literal:** validate exactly what the YAML says, nothing more.

Which do you want?

package com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory

import com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.TestcontainersConfiguration

/**
 * `GET /inventory`, `GET /inventory/{skuId}`, `POST /inventory/{skuId}` and
 * `POST /inventory/{skuId}/purchase` through the full stack
 * (MockMvc → controller → service → repository → Postgres), checking status, content type and the
 * exact body (JSON on success, plain text on 400/404) against the contract, and that rejected
 * requests leave stock unchanged.
 */
@SpringBootTest
@AutoConfigureMockMvc
@Import(TestcontainersConfiguration::class)
class InventoryControllerTest @Autowired constructor(
    private val mockMvc: MockMvc,
    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,
) {

    /** Starts every test from an empty table, since all tests in this context share one database. */
    @BeforeEach
    fun clearInventory() {
        jdbcClient.sql("DELETE FROM inventory").update()
    }

    private fun insert(skuId: String, quantity: Int) {
        jdbcClient.sql("INSERT INTO inventory (sku_id, quantity) VALUES (?, ?)")
            .params(skuId, quantity)
            .update()
    }

    private fun quantityOf(skuId: String): Int? =
        jdbcClient.sql("SELECT quantity FROM inventory WHERE sku_id = ?")
            .param(skuId)
            .query { rs, _ -> rs.getInt("quantity") }
            .list()
            .singleOrNull()

        skuId: String,
        body: String,
        mediaType: MediaType = MediaType.APPLICATION_JSON,

```json
{
  "file_path": "C:\\Users\\rober\\Documents\\Projects\\Nuuly_Inventory_Assessment\\src\\test\\kotlin\\com\\robertwoolbright\\inventory\\inventory\\InventoryContractTest.kt",
  "content": "// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/inventory/InventoryContractTest.kt.md\npackage com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory\n\nimport com.atlassian.oai.validator.OpenApiInteractionValidator\nimport com.atlassian.oai.validator.mockmvc.OpenApiValidationMatchers.openApi\nimport com.atlassian.oai.validator.model.Request\nimport com.atlassian.oai.validator.model.SimpleResponse\nimport com.atlassian.oai.validator.report.LevelResolver\nimport com.atlassian.oai.validator.report.ValidationReport\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.TestcontainersConfiguration\nimport io.swagger.v3.oas.models.OpenAPI\nimport io.swagger.v3.parser.OpenAPIV3Parser\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.BeforeEach\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.Test\nimport org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Autowired\nimport org.springframework.boot.test.context.SpringBootTest\nimport org.springframework.boot.webmvc.test.autoconfigure.AutoConfigureMockMvc\nimport org.springframework.context.annotation.Import\nimport org.springframework.http.MediaType\nimport org.springframework.jdbc.core.simple.JdbcClient\nimport org.springframework.test.web.servlet.MockMvc\nimport org.springframework.test.web.servlet.ResultActionsDsl\nimport org.springframework.test.web.servlet.get\nimport org.springframework.test.web.servlet.post\nimport kotlin.test.assertFalse\nimport kotlin.test.assertTrue\n\n/**\n * Contract validation: every response each endpoint can give, checked against inventory.yaml by\n * Atlassian's OpenAPI validator (declared status, content type, and body schema). `InventoryItem`\n * is checked strictly: both fields required, no extra fields. Also proves the strict schema\n * rejects bodies that break it.\n */\n@SpringBootTest\n@AutoConfigureMockMvc\n@Import(TestcontainersConfiguration::class)\nclass InventoryContractTest @Autowired constructor(\n    private val mockMvc: MockMvc,\n    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,\n) {\n\n    /** Validates the request and the response; for requests that follow the contract. */\n    private val validator = OpenApiInteractionValidator.createFor(strictContract()).build()\n\n    // Why: the 400 tests send invalid requests on purpose. <details><summary>Claude reasoning</summary>

The flaw was in my experiment, not the test: requiring made-up fields like `price` gets silently ignored since only defined properties are enforced, and extra fields are already rejected by Atlassian's default, making my `additionalProperties = false` redundant. I'll rerun the experiment properly using a constraint the app's actual response violates (quantity max 2 vs. the returned 5).

</details>

65:| Contract gaps (POST) | Settled for `POST /inventory/{skuId}` by Robert on 2026-09-28. **SKU ids: exact, case-sensitive match** (`W` and `w` are two SKUs; the contract sets no format, and normalizing would silently change the id a client sent). **Strict JSON:** malformed, empty or `null` bodies, a missing or `null` `quantity`, a string (`"5"`), a decimal (`1.5`) or a number too big for `Int` are all `400` `Invalid request body` (Jackson's lenient defaults would coerce `"5"` and truncate `1.5`). `quantity < 1`: `400` `quantity must be at least 1`, checked with Bean Validation in the controller layer (considered a manual check in the service). The service also has `require(quantity >= 1)` for callers that bypass HTTP; hitting it is our bug, so it's a `500`, not a `400` (added in review, 2026-09-28). **Wrong `Content-Type`:** `400` `Content-Type must be application/json` instead of Spring's `415`, which the contract doesn't list. **Overflow:** a total past `Int.MAX_VALUE` is `400` `Quantity would exceed the maximum stock`, refused atomically inside the upsert's SQL (considered leaving Postgres's `integer out of range` as a `500`). All `400` bodies are `text/plain`. | Decided 2026-09-28 |
66:| Contract gaps (purchase) | Settled for `POST /inventory/{skuId}/purchase` by Robert on 2026-09-29. **One SQL statement decides the outcome:** a data-modifying CTE (`WITH purchased AS (UPDATE ... WHERE quantity >= ? RETURNING quantity) SELECT remaining, EXISTS(...)`) deducts the stock and reports whether the SKU exists, and the repository returns a sealed `PurchaseResult` (`Purchased`, `SkuNotFound`, `InsufficientStock`). Considered an `UPDATE` followed by a lookup when it changed nothing (the SKU can change between the two statements) and `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` in a transaction (correct, but holds the row lock for three round trips). **Insufficient stock:** `400` `Insufficient inventory for <skuId>`, no partial fill, stock unchanged. A sold-out SKU (quantity `0`) is insufficient stock, not a missing SKU. **Missing SKU:** `404` `SKU not found: <skuId>`, the same body as `GET /inventory/{skuId}`; a purchase never creates a SKU. **Invalid body:** the same four `400`s as `POST /inventory/{skuId}` (same `InventoryQuantity` schema). The body is validated before the SKU is looked up, so an invalid body for a missing SKU is a `400`, not a `404`. The service's `require(quantity >= 1)` also blocks direct callers, since a negative purchase would add stock. | Decided 2026-09-29 |

### Test dependencies

| Dependency | What it gives us |
| --- | --- |
| `spring-boot-starter-{flyway,jdbc,webmvc}-test` | Test support for each module, e.g. `@WebMvcTest` + `MockMvc` and `@JdbcTest`. They also pull in `spring-boot-starter-test`: JUnit Jupiter 6, AssertJ, Mockito. |
| `spring-boot-testcontainers` | `@ServiceConnection`, which turns a running container into DataSource settings. |
| `kotlin-test-junit5` | `kotlin.test` assertions (`assertEquals`, `assertFailsWith`) backed by JUnit Jupiter. The artifact keeps its old `junit5` name, but it runs fine on JUnit 6. |
| `testcontainers-junit-jupiter`, `testcontainers-postgresql` | Start a real Postgres in Docker for tests. |
| `junit-platform-launcher` | Gradle 9 no longer bundles the JUnit Platform launcher; it has to be on the test runtime classpath. |

**Why:** tests run against real Postgres instead of H2, an in-memory database that imitates
others. Our oversell protection depends on Postgres-specific behavior (`ON CONFLICT`, row locks
during `UPDATE`, `CHECK` constraints), and H2 imitates those imperfectly.

### Compiler options
## Open decisions

These are settled one at a time before the code that depends on them is written.
Each decision and its reasoning is recorded here once it is made.

| Decision | Options under consideration | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Data store | **PostgreSQL**, run with Docker Compose. Considered in-memory and H2; see below. | Decided 2026-09-23 |
| Concurrency on purchase | **Single atomic SQL statements** + `CHECK (quantity >= 0)` backstop. See below. | Decided 2026-09-23 |
| Data-access library | **`JdbcClient`**: explicit SQL, maps to immutable data classes. Considered Spring Data JDBC, Spring Data JPA, Exposed. | Decided 2026-09-23 |
| Schema migrations | **Flyway**: versioned plain-SQL files, run automatically at startup and in tests. Considered Liquibase (XML/YAML changesets, more ceremony) and `schema.sql` (no versioning). | De
| List order and contents | **Ordered by `sku_id`** (the contract specifies no order; a fixed one keeps responses and tests stable). **Zero-stock SKUs are listed**: a sold-out SKU still exists, and `G
| Column types | `sku_id TEXT` (exact, case-sensitive match for now), `quantity INTEGER` (Kotlin `Int`). Considered `BIGINT`/`Long`; overflow will be handled when adding stock instead. | Decided 2026-
| Error handling | A domain exception per error (`SkuNotFoundException`, `AddStockOverflowException`, `InsufficientInventoryException`), thrown by the service and mapped to a `text/plain` response by 
| Demo seed data | Tests insert their own rows. Whether a running app should start with sample data (e.g. a dev-only Flyway location) is decided once `POST /inventory/{skuId}` exists. | Open |

### Claude

Part (e), the contract-validation test, is done: the full suite is 117 tests, all passing (100 before, plus 17 new).

**What `InventoryContractTest` checks:**
- **Every declared response:** ten tests call the real app, one for each status of each endpoint in `inventory.yaml`, and Atlassian's validator checks the status, content type and body against the YAML.
- **Strict `InventoryItem`:** the test adds `required: [skuId, quantity]` to its parsed copy of the YAML. The file itself isn't changed.
- **Seven self-checks:** a correct item passes; a missing field, an extra field, a negative quantity, an error sent as JSON, an item sent as plain text, and a 500 are each rejected for the specific reason expected.

**I tested the test, and my first experiment was flawed.** To prove the validator catches bad app responses, I required a made-up field, `price`. Everything stayed green. Probing showed the validator only enforces `required` for fields the schema actually defines, so the experiment proved nothing.

Tightening a real rule instead (`quantity` at most 2) made exactly the four 200 tests with larger quantities fail, which confirmed the checks work. That's why each self-check now asserts the specific error instead of just "some error". It's the same lesson as your negative control, and it's written up in the notes with a check question.

**Things I learned about the validator along the way:**
- It rejects undeclared fields by default, so my first "no extra fields" line did nothing. I removed it and recorded the real reason in the notes.
- The two invalid-quantity tests have to skip request checking. With the full validator, exactly those two fail, so the second validator is needed.
- It accepts `text/plain;charset=UTF-8`. I noted that on the open charset decision in the TODO.

**Changed files:**
- **New dependency:** `com.atlassian.oai:openapi-request-validator-mockmvc:3.0.0` in [build.gradle.kts](build.gradle.kts).
- **Docs:** notes for the contract test and the build file, five entries in the concepts index, a "Contract validation" decision row in the README, and the TODO item checked off.

**Proposed commit:** `Add contract-validation test against inventory.yaml`, ending with the `Co-Authored-By` line.

Want to read through the test first, or should I commit? After this comes part (d), the property-based test.

## Next: part (d), the property-based test

**What it is:** a normal test checks the examples you thought of. A property-based test states a **rule** and lets a library generate hundreds of random cases, looking for one that breaks it. When it finds one, it shrinks it down to the smallest case that still fails.

**How it would work here:**
1. Kotest generates a random list of operations, such as `receive("b", 4)`, `purchase("a", 3)`, `purchase("c", 1)`, on a few SKUs.
2. Each operation runs against the **real repository** (Postgres) and against a simple **in-memory model**: a `Map<String, Int>` that obviously does the right thing.
3. After every step, the two must agree: the same `PurchaseResult`, the same stock. No SKU ever goes negative.

**What's new for you:**
- Kotest's `Arb` generators, `checkAll` and shrinking.
- A first small taste of coroutines: `checkAll` is a `suspend` function, so the test wraps it in `runBlocking`.
- Model-based testing as an idea.

When we planned this you chose to write only the naive repository, so I'd write all of this. The in-memory model is still a good small Kotlin exercise, about 15 lines with a `mutableMapOf` and the same `PurchaseResult` you already know. Should I write it all, or do you want to write the model?

{
  "file_path": "C:\\Users\\rober\\Documents\\Projects\\Nuuly_Inventory_Assessment\\src\\test\\kotlin\\com\\robertwoolbright\\inventory\\inventory\\InventoryPropertyTest.kt",
  "content": "// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/inventory/InventoryPropertyTest.kt.md\npackage com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory\n\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.TestcontainersConfiguration\nimport io.kotest.property.Arb\nimport io.kotest.property.PropTestConfig\nimport io.kotest.property.arbitrary.bind\nimport io.kotest.property.arbitrary.choice\nimport io.kotest.property.arbitrary.element\nimport io.kotest.property.arbitrary.int\nimport io.kotest.property.arbitrary.list\nimport io.kotest.property.checkAll\nimport kotlinx.coroutines.runBlocking\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.Test\nimport org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Autowired\nimport org.springframework.boot.jdbc.test.autoconfigure.JdbcTest\nimport org.springframework.context.annotation.Import\nimport org.springframework.jdbc.core.simple.JdbcClient\nimport kotlin.test.assertEquals\nimport kotlin.test.assertTrue\n\n/**\n * Oversell test plan, model-based: random sequences of receives and purchases run against\n * [InventoryRepository] (real Postgres) and against a simple in-memory model at the same time.\n * After every step both must give the same answer and hold the same stock, and no SKU may ever go\n * negative. Single-threaded: the race tests cover concurrency.\n */\n@JdbcTest\n@Import(TestcontainersConfiguration::class, InventoryRepository::class)\nclass InventoryPropertyTest @Autowired constructor(\n    private val inventoryRepository: InventoryRepository,\n    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,\n) {\n\n    /** One step of a generated sequence. */\n    private sealed interface Operation {\n        val skuId: String\n        val quantity: Int\n\n        data class Receive(override val skuId: String, override val quantity: Int) : Operation\n        data class Purchase(override val skuId: String, override val quantity: Int) : Operation\n    }\n\n    // Why: the obviously-correct version of the rules, with no SQL and no concurrency. If the\n    // repository ever disagrees with it, one of the two is wrong, and the model is easy to check by eye.\n    /** In-memory inventory: a map from SKU to stock, following the same rules as the contract. */\n    private class InventoryModel {\n        private val stock = mutableMapOf<String, Int>()\n\n        fun receive(skuId: String, quantity: Int): InventoryItem {\n            val newQuantity = stock.getOrDefault(skuId, 0) + quantity\n            stock[skuId] = newQuantity\n            return InventoryItem(skuId, newQuantity)\n        }\n\n        fun purchase(skuId: String, quantity: Int): PurchaseResult {\n            val current = stock[skuId] ?: return PurchaseResult.SkuNotFound\n            if (current < quantity) return PurchaseResult.InsufficientStock\n            stock[skuId] = current - quantity\n            return PurchaseResult.Purchased(InventoryItem(skuId, current - quantity))\n        }\n\n        fun items(): List<InventoryItem> = stock.map { (skuId, quantity) -> InventoryItem(skuId, quantity) }.sortedBy { it.skuId }\n    }\n\n    // Why: three SKUs so operations collide on the same row often; purchases up to 15 against\n    // receives up to 10, so \"insufficient\" is common; \"c\" is never received, so \"not found\" happens too.\n    private val skuIds = Arb.element(\"a\", \"b\", \"c\")\n    private val operation: Arb<Operation> = Arb.choice(\n        Arb.bind(Arb.element(\"a\", \"b\"), Arb.int(1..10)) { skuId, quantity -> Operation.Receive(skuId, quantity) },\n        Arb.bind(skuIds, Arb.int(1..15)) { skuId, quantity -> Operation.Purchase(skuId, quantity) },\n    )\n\n    /**\n     * For any sequence of up to 30 operations, the repository and the model agree after every step,\n     * and stock never goes negative.\n     */\n    @Test\n    fun `the repository behaves like the in-memory model for any

```
    // Why: a block body, not `= runBlocking { ... }`. That form would return checkAll's result, and
    // JUnit silently skips @Test methods that don't return Unit.
    @Test
    fun `the repository behaves like the in-memory model for any sequence of operations`() {
        runBlocking {
            checkAll(PropTestConfig(iterations = 200), Arb.list(operation, 1..30)) { operations ->
                jdbcClient.sql("DELETE FROM inventory").update()
                val model = InventoryModel()

                operations.forEachIndexed { step, operation ->
                    val context = "step $step of $operations"
                    when (operation) {
                        is Operation.Receive -> assertEquals(
                            model.receive(operation.skuId, operation.quantity),
                            inventoryRepository.addStock(operation.skuId, operation.quantity),
                            "receive at $context",
                        )
                        is Operation.Purchase -> assertEquals(
                            model.purchase(operation.skuId, operation.quantity),
                            inventoryRepository.removeStock(operation.skuId, operation.quantity),
                            "purchase at $context",
                        )
                    }
                    val stored = inventoryRepository.findAll()
                    assertEquals(model.items(), stored, "stock after $context")
                    assertTrue(stored.all { it.quantity >= 0 }, "negative stock after $context: $stored")
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
```

### Claude

Part (d), the property-based test, is done: 118 tests, all green.

**What `InventoryPropertyTest` does:**
- Kotest generates 200 random sequences of up to 30 receives and purchases across three SKUs.
- Each sequence runs against the real repository in Postgres and against a small in-memory model.
- After every step, both must return the same result and hold the same stock, and nothing may go negative.
- The generator ranges are tuned so "purchased", "insufficient stock" and "not found" all come up often.

**Testing the test:** I broke the model on purpose so it refused to sell the last unit. Kotest caught it on attempt 4 and shrank a 3-step sequence down to the smallest case that still fails: "receive 9, then buy 9". It also printed a seed to replay that exact run. That output is quoted in the study notes as a worked example of shrinking.

**Two Kotlin lessons from this part:**
- **`fun test() = runBlocking { ... }` silently doesn't run.** With `=`, the function returns `checkAll`'s result, JUnit skips any `@Test` that doesn't return `Unit`, and Gradle said "No tests found". It's the same family as your `= { }` trap, and there's a check question on it.
- **What this test can't catch:** your naive repository would pass it, because it's correct when calls take turns. That's why the plan needs both this test and the race tests.

**Changed files:**
- `io.kotest:kotest-property:6.2.5` in `build.gradle.kts` (the property module only, so the test stays a normal JUnit test).
- The test file and its study notes.
- The build-file notes, six new entries in the concepts index, and the TODO item checked off.

**Proposed commit:** `Add property-based test against an in-memory model`. Should I commit it and go straight on to (b), the two-app-instances race?

// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/support/TestcontainersConfiguration.kt.md
package com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support

import org.springframework.boot.test.context.TestConfiguration
import org.springframework.boot.testcontainers.service.connection.ServiceConnection
import org.springframework.context.annotation.Bean
import org.testcontainers.postgresql.PostgreSQLContainer
import org.testcontainers.utility.DockerImageName

/**
 * Shared test setup: a real PostgreSQL database in a throwaway Docker container.
 *
 * Tests that `@Import` this class get a DataSource pointing at the container. Spring caches
 * the test context, so tests with the same setup share one container.
 */
@TestConfiguration(proxyBeanMethods = false)
class TestcontainersConfiguration {

    /** The Postgres container behind every integration test; its connection details become the DataSource. */
    @Bean
    @ServiceConnection
    fun postgresContainer(): PostgreSQLContainer =
        // Why: same major version as compose.yaml, so tests and local runs behave the same.
        PostgreSQLContainer(DockerImageName.parse("postgres:18"))
}
# Study notes: docs/learning/notes/main/resources/application.yml.md
# Why: no spring.datasource.* here. Docker Compose support (bootRun) and Testcontainers'
# @ServiceConnection (tests) supply the connection details, so no credentials live in config.
spring:
  # Why: Jackson's lenient defaults silently turn "5" into 5 and 1.5 into 1. Stock counts
  # must be exact, so those bodies are a 400 instead.
  jackson:
    deserialization:
      accept-float-as-int: false
    mapper:
      allow-coercion-of-scalars: false
  application:
    name: inventory
// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/main/InventoryApplication.kt.md
package com.robertwoolbright.inventory

import org.springframework.boot.autoconfigure.SpringBootApplication
import org.springframework.boot.runApplication

/**
 * Entry point of the inventory service. Component scanning starts from this package, so every
 * controller, service and repository below it is picked up automatically.
 */
@SpringBootApplication
class InventoryApplication

/** Starts Spring: builds the application context, runs Flyway migrations and starts Tomcat. */
fun main(args: Array<String>) {
    runApplication<InventoryApplication>(*args)
}
# Study notes: docs/learning/notes/compose.yaml.md
# Local Postgres for `./gradlew bootRun`. Spring Boot's Docker Compose support starts and stops
# it automatically. Tests don't use this file; they use Testcontainers.
services:
  postgres:
    # Why: pinned major version, kept in sync with support/TestcontainersConfiguration.kt.
    image: 'postgres:18'
    environment:
      - 'POSTGRES_DB=inventory'
      - 'POSTGRES_USER=inventory'
      - 'POSTGRES_PASSWORD=inventory'
    ports:
      # Why: random free host port (no clash with a local Postgres), bound to 127.0.0.1 only so the
      # dev database isn't reachable from the network. Plain '5432' would bind on all interfaces.
      - '127.0.0.1::5432'
```

  "content": "// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/support/InventoryHttpClient.kt.md\npackage com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support\n\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory.InventoryItem\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory.PurchaseResult\nimport tools.jackson.module.kotlin.jacksonObjectMapper\nimport tools.jackson.module.kotlin.readValue\nimport java.net.URI\nimport java.net.http.HttpClient\nimport java.net.http.HttpRequest\nimport java.net.http.HttpResponse\nimport java.time.Duration\n\n/**\n * Calls a running inventory app over real HTTP, for tests that need more than MockMvc: several app\n * instances, or real sockets under load. Answers come back as the repository's types\n * ([InventoryItem], [PurchaseResult]), so HTTP results can go through the same assertions.\n * Safe to share between threads.\n */\nclass InventoryHttpClient(private val baseUrl: String) {\n\n    // Why: HTTP/1.1 like a typical client; the JDK's default would first try an HTTP/2 upgrade.\n    private val http = HttpClient.newBuilder()\n        .version(HttpClient.Version.HTTP_1_1)\n        .connectTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(5))\n        .build()\n    private val json = jacksonObjectMapper()\n\n    /** `POST /inventory/{skuId}`: adds stock and returns the new total; fails on anything but 200. */\n    fun receive(skuId: String, quantity: Int): InventoryItem {\n        val response = post(\"/inventory/$skuId\", quantity)\n        check(response.statusCode() == 200) { \"receive $skuId: ${response.statusCode()} ${response.body()}\" }\n        return json.readValue(response.body())\n    }\n\n    // Why: 400 is also the status for an invalid body, so the message decides; anything unexpected\n    // throws instead of being counted as a refused purchase.\n    /** `POST /inventory/{skuId}/purchase`, with the response translated back into a [PurchaseResult]. */\n    fun purchase(skuId: String, quantity: Int): PurchaseResult {\n        val response = post(\"/inventory/$skuId/purchase\", quantity)\n        return when {\n            response.statusCode() == 200 -> PurchaseResult.Purchased(json.readValue(response.body()))\n            response.statusCode() == 400 && response.body().startsWith(\"Insufficient inventory\") ->\n                PurchaseResult.InsufficientStock\n            response.statusCode() == 404 -> PurchaseResult.SkuNotFound\n            else -> error(\"purchase $skuId: ${response.statusCode()} ${response.body()}\")\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** `GET /inventory/{skuId}`: the SKU's stock, or `null` on a 404. */\n    fun quantityOf(skuId: String): Int? {\n        val response = http.send(request(\"/inventory/$skuId\").GET().build(), HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString())\n        return when (response.statusCode()) {\n            200 -> json.readValue<InventoryItem>(response.body()).quantity\n            404 -> null\n            else -> error(\"get $skuId: ${response.statusCode()} ${response.body()}\")\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** `GET /inventory`: every SKU, in the order the app returns them. */\n    fun inventory(): List<InventoryItem> {\n        val response = http.send(request(\"/inventory\").GET().build(), HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString())\n        check(response.statusCode() == 200) { \"list: ${response.statusCode()} ${response.body()}\" }\n        return json.readValue(response.body())\n    }\n\n    private fun post(path: String, quantity: Int): HttpResponse<String> =\n        http.send(\n            request(path)\n                .header(\"Content-Type\", \"application/json\")\n                .POST(HttpRequest.BodyPublishers.ofString(\"\"\"{\"quantity\": $quantity}\"\"\"))\n                .build(),\n            HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString(),\n        )\n\n    private fun request(path: String): HttpRequest.Builder =\n        HttpRequest.

  "content": "// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/inventory/MultiInstanceRaceTest.kt.md\npackage com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory\n\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.InventoryApplication\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.InventoryHttpClient\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.race\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.AfterAll\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.BeforeAll\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.BeforeEach\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.RepeatedTest\nimport org.springframework.boot.builder.SpringApplicationBuilder\nimport org.springframework.context.ConfigurableApplicationContext\nimport org.springframework.jdbc.core.simple.JdbcClient\nimport org.testcontainers.postgresql.PostgreSQLContainer\nimport org.testcontainers.utility.DockerImageName\nimport kotlin.test.assertEquals\nimport kotlin.test.assertTrue\n\n// Why: two full application contexts, each with its own Tomcat port and connection pool, like two\n// pods behind a load balancer. Locks inside one JVM can't protect stock here; only the database can.\n/**\n * Oversell test plan, across instances: two copies of the app share one Postgres, and buyers split\n * between them race over real HTTP. No purchase may oversell and no update may be lost, because the\n * guarantee lives in the database, not in either app.\n */\nclass MultiInstanceRaceTest {\n\n    /** Starts every test from an empty table; both instances see the same one. */\n    @BeforeEach\n    fun clearInventory() {\n        jdbcClient.sql(\"DELETE FROM inventory\").update()\n    }\n\n    /** Buyers of 1 alternate between the instances; the oversell check is the same as for one instance. */\n    @RepeatedTest(3)\n    fun `purchases split across two instances never oversell`() {\n        val stock = 10\n        instanceA.receive(\"widget\", stock)\n        val buyers = 50\n\n        val results = race(buyers) { i -> instances[i % 2].purchase(\"widget\", 1) }\n\n        val failures = results.mapNotNull { it.exceptionOrNull() }\n        assertTrue(failures.isEmpty(), \"purchases failed: $failures\")\n        assertNoOversell(stock, results.map { it.getOrThrow() }, instanceB.quantityOf(\"widget\"))\n    }\n\n    // Why: restocks go only to A and purchases only to B, so every interleaving of the two apps'\n    // writes to the one row is exercised. Demand (30 x 5 = 150) exceeds supply (20 + 30 x 3 = 110).\n    /** Restocks through one instance and purchases through the other keep an exact balance. */\n    @RepeatedTest(3)\n    fun `restocks on one instance and purchases on the other keep an exact balance`() {\n        val initial = 20\n        instanceA.receive(\"widget\", initial)\n        val threads = 60\n\n        val results = race(threads) { i ->\n            if (i % 2 == 0) {\n                instanceA.receive(\"widget\", 3)\n                3\n            } else {\n                when (instanceB.purchase(\"widget\", 5)) {\n                    is PurchaseResult.Purchased -> -5\n                    PurchaseResult.InsufficientStock -> 0\n                    PurchaseResult.SkuNotFound -> error(\"an existing SKU was reported missing\")\n                }\n            }\n        }\n\n        val failures = results.mapNotNull { it.exceptionOrNull() }\n        assertTrue(failures.isEmpty(), \"operations failed: $failures\")\n        assertEquals(initial + results.sumOf { it.getOrThrow() }, instanceA.quantityOf(\"widget\"))\n    }\n\n    private companion object {\n        private val postgres = PostgreSQLContainer(DockerImageName.parse(\"postgres:18\"))\n        private lateinit var appA: ConfigurableApplicationContext\n        private lateinit var appB: ConfigurableApplicationContext\n        private lateinit var instanceA: InventoryHttpClient\n        private lateinit var instanceB: InventoryHttpClient\n

One\nclient is thread-safe and keeps a pool of connections, so fifty racing threads share it.\n`send(request, BodyHandlers.ofString())` blocks until the response arrives and gives the body as a\n`String`.\n\n**Why HTTP/1.1:** the JDK client defaults to HTTP/2 and, over plain `http://`, first sends an\n\"upgrade\" request. Tomcat doesn't enable that by default, so every call would pay for a refused\nupgrade. Anything that isn't one of the three expected answers throws, and `race` reports it as\na failure.\n\n**Kotlin: `readValue` with an inferred type.** `json.readValue(response.body())` is a reified generic\nfunction (see the concepts index). In production a service runs as several copies behind a\nload balancer. Anything that only works inside one JVM, such as a `synchronized` block, a Kotlin\n`Mutex` or an in-memory cache of stock, breaks as soon as there are two. This test starts two\ncomplete copies of the app against one Postgres and races buyers across both over real HTTP.\n\nIt passes because the protection was never in the app: it's the single SQL statement and the\nrow lock in Postgres (see the README decision record).\n\n## Walkthrough\n\n### No Spring test annotations\n\n```kotlin\n// Why: two full application contexts, each with its own Tomcat port and connection pool, like two\n// pods behind a load balancer. Locks inside one JVM can't protect stock here; only the database can.\n...\nclass MultiInstanceRaceTest {\n```\n\n**Spring:** `@SpringBootTest` gives a test *one* application context. This test needs two, so it\nstarts them itself (below) and is a plain JUnit class. Spring's test context cache doesn't know\nabout these contexts, which is why the class closes them in `@AfterAll`.\n\n### Starting one database and two apps\n\n```kotlin\nprivate companion object {\n    private val postgres = PostgreSQLContainer(DockerImageName.parse(\"postgres:18\"))\n    private lateinit var appA: ConfigurableApplicationContext\n    private lateinit var appB: ConfigurableApplicationContext\n...\n    /** One database, then two apps pointed at it; the second finds Flyway's migration already applied. */\n    @BeforeAll\n    @JvmStatic\n    fun startInstances() {\n        postgres.start()\n        appA = startApp()\n        appB = startApp()\n```\n\n**JUnit: `@BeforeAll` / `@AfterAll`.** Run once for the whole class, not per test. Starting two\napps takes a few seconds, so all tests share them; `@BeforeEach` only empties the table. JUnit\nrequires these methods to be *static* (it calls them before any test instance exists).\n\n**Kotlin: `companion object` + `@JvmStatic`.** Kotlin has no `static` (see\n[InventoryContractTest.kt](InventoryContractTest.kt.md)). A companion object's functions are\ncalled on the class, but in bytecode they live on a separate object. `@JvmStatic` also generates a\nreal static method on the class, which is what JUnit looks for.\n\n**Kotlin: `lateinit var`.** A non-null property that is assigned later, not in a constructor. ### Claude

Part (b), the multi-instance race, is done: the full suite is 124 tests, all green, and the whole build takes about 30 seconds.

**What `MultiInstanceRaceTest` does:**
- It starts one Postgres container and two complete copies of the app with `SpringApplicationBuilder`. Each has its own random port, Tomcat and connection pool, like two pods behind a load balancer.
- **Purchase race:** 50 buyers alternate between the two apps over real HTTP for 10 units. The result goes through the same `assertNoOversell`, and the final stock is read back through the *other* app.
- **Mixed race:** all restocks go to app A and all purchases to app B, and the stock has to balance exactly.
- Both tests repeat 3 times.

**Why it matters in an interview:** a fix done in Kotlin, like a `synchronized` block around a read-then-write, passes the single-app race tests but fails this one, because each app has its own lock. The fix has to live in the database, which is where it lives here. That's one of the check questions in the notes.

**New shared helper:** `support/InventoryHttpClient.kt` translates HTTP answers back into `PurchaseResult`, so HTTP races reuse the same assertion. It only treats a 400 as "insufficient" if the body says so; any other 400 fails the test instead of being counted as a refused purchase. The load test will reuse this helper.

**New Kotlin concepts:** `lateinit var`, `@JvmStatic` for `@BeforeAll`/`@AfterAll`, and the JDK `HttpClient`. All three are in the notes and the concepts index.

**Proposed commit:** `Add race test across two app instances`. Should I commit it? After that, the last test part is (c), the HTTP load test with its own `./gradlew loadTest` task, followed by the README fixes.
