  "content": "// Study notes: docs/learning/notes/test/inventory/InventoryLoadTest.kt.md\npackage com.robertwoolbright.inventory.inventory\n\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.InventoryHttpClient\nimport com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.TestcontainersConfiguration\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.Tag\nimport org.junit.jupiter.api.Test\nimport org.springframework.beans.factory.annotation.Autowired\nimport org.springframework.boot.test.context.SpringBootTest\nimport org.springframework.boot.test.web.server.LocalServerPort\nimport org.springframework.context.annotation.Import\nimport org.springframework.jdbc.core.simple.JdbcClient\nimport java.util.concurrent.Callable\nimport java.util.concurrent.Executors\nimport java.util.concurrent.TimeUnit\nimport kotlin.random.Random\nimport kotlin.test.assertEquals\nimport kotlin.test.assertTrue\n\n/**\n * Oversell test plan, HTTP load test: many clients buy and restock a few popular SKUs over real\n * HTTP for a fixed time, then the final stock of each SKU must equal what was received minus what\n * was sold, and never be negative. Prints throughput and latency. Tagged `load`: it runs with\n * `./gradlew loadTest`, not with `./gradlew test`.\n */\n@Tag(\"load\")\n@SpringBootTest(webEnvironment = SpringBootTest.WebEnvironment.RANDOM_PORT)\n@Import(TestcontainersConfiguration::class)\nclass InventoryLoadTest @Autowired constructor(\n    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,\n    @LocalServerPort private val port: Int,\n) {\n\n    private val seconds = System.getProperty(\"load.seconds\")?.toLong() ?: 20\n    private val clients = System.getProperty(\"load.clients\")?.toInt() ?: 50\n    private val hotSkus = listOf(\"hot-1\", \"hot-2\", \"hot-3\")\n    private val initialStock = 100\n\n    /** What one client did: units sold and received per SKU, outcome counts, and every latency. */\n    private class Tally {\n        val sold = mutableMapOf<String, Int>()\n        val received = mutableMapOf<String, Int>()\n        var purchased = 0\n        var refused = 0\n        var restocks = 0\n        val latenciesMicros = mutableListOf<Long>()\n    }\n\n    // Why: demand (70% of calls, 1-3 units) outruns supply (30%, 1-3 units), so every SKU spends\n    // most of the run near zero, where an oversell would happen, instead of far from it.\n    /** Sustained purchases and restocks on three SKUs; the books must balance exactly at the end. */\n    @Test\n    fun `sustained load on hot SKUs never oversells and keeps an exact balance`() {\n        jdbcClient.sql(\"DELETE FROM inventory\").update()\n        val client = InventoryHttpClient(\"http://localhost:$port\")\n        hotSkus.forEach { client.receive(it, initialStock) }\n        val deadline = System.nanoTime() + TimeUnit.SECONDS.toNanos(seconds)\n\n        val started = System.nanoTime()\n        val tallies = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor().use { executor ->\n            (0 until clients)\n                .map { i -> executor.submit(Callable { runClient(client, Random(i), deadline) }) }\n                .map { it.get() }\n        }\n        val elapsedSeconds = (System.nanoTime() - started) / 1e9\n\n        val stored = client.inventory().associate { it.skuId to it.quantity }\n        for (sku in hotSkus) {\n            val received = tallies.sumOf { it.received[sku] ?: 0 }\n            val sold = tallies.sumOf { it.sold[sku] ?: 0 }\n            assertEquals(initialStock + received - sold, stored[sku], \"balance of $sku\")\n            assertTrue(stored.getValue(sku) >= 0, \"$sku went negative: ${stored[sku]}\")\n        }\n        val purchased = tallies.sumOf { it.purchased }\n        val refused = tallies.sumOf { it.refused }\n        assertTrue(purchased > 0 && refused > 0, \"the run never reached the stock limit: $purchased sold, $refused refused\

````
### Tests run on the JUnit Platform

```kotlin
tasks.withType<Test> {
    useJUnitPlatform()
}
```

Spring Boot 4 manages **JUnit 6**. The test API we write against (`org.junit.jupiter.api.Test`)
is the same Jupiter API as JUnit 5. `useJUnitPlatform()` tells Gradle to run tests through the
JUnit Platform rather than the legacy JUnit 4 runner.

**Kotlin:** `withType<Test>` uses a **reified type parameter**. On the JVM, generic types are
normally erased at runtime, but an `inline` function can mark its type parameter `reified` and
use it as a real class. So you write `withType<Test>` instead of Java's
`withType(Test.class)`. The trailing lambda then configures every `Test` task.

## Check your understanding
## Check your understanding

- Why does `postgresql` use `runtimeOnly` and not `implementation`? What would break if it were `compileOnly`?
- Remove `kotlin("plugin.spring")`. Which kind of class would Spring fail on, and why?
- `spring-boot-starter-validation` has no version in this file. Where does Gradle get one, and how would you check which it picked?
- What does `-Xjsr305=strict` change about a Java method annotated `@Nullable String find()` when called from Kotlin?
````

</details>

<details><summary>Tool call: Bash — Document the loadTest task in build notes</summary>

````json
{
  "command": "cd \"C:/Users/rober/Documents/Projects/Nuuly_Inventory_Assessment\" && python - <<'EOF'\np=\"docs/learning/notes/build.gradle.kts.md\"\ns=open(p,encoding=\"utf-8\").read()\nanchor=\"use it as a real class. So you write `withType<Test>` instead of Java's\\n`withType(Test.class)`. The trailing lambda then configures every `Test` task.\\n\"\nassert anchor in s\ns=s.replace(anchor, anchor+'''\n### A separate task for the load test\n\n```kotlin\n// Why: the load test runs for a fixed time (20 s by default), too slow for every build, so it has\n// its own task. Considered always running it: that would make every `./gradlew build` slower.\ntasks.test {\n    useJUnitPlatform { excludeTags(\"load\") }\n}\n\n// Runs only the @Tag(\"load\") tests: ./gradlew loadTest [-Dload.seconds=60 -Dload.clients=100]\ntasks.register<Test>(\"loadTest\") {\n    description = \"Runs the HTTP load test against the real app.\"\n    group = \"verification\"\n    testClassesDirs = sourceSets.test.get().output.classesDirs\n    classpath = sourceSets.test.get().runtimeClasspath\n    useJUnitPlatform { includeTags(\"load\") }\n    shouldRunAfter(tasks.test)\n    // Why: a load test is a measurement; rerun it every time instead of reusing the last result.\n    outputs.upToDateWhen { false }\n    testLogging.showStandardStreams = true\n    for (key in listOf(\"load.seconds\", \"load.clients\")) {\n        providers.systemProperty(key).orNull?.let { systemProperty(key, it) }\n    }\n}\n```\n\nAdded 2026-09-30 for [InventoryLoadTest.kt](test/inventory/InventoryLoadTest.kt.md) (Robert chose a\nseparate task over running it in every build).\n\n**JUnit: tags.** `@Tag(\"load\")` on a test class labels it. `excludeTags(\"load\")` keeps it out of\nthe normal `test` task; `includeTags(\"load\")` makes the new task run *only* tagged tests.\n\n**Gradle: registering a task.** `tasks.register<Test>(\"loadTest\") { ... }` creates a second task of\nthe same type as `test`. A new `Test` task doesn't know where the tests are, so it gets the test\nsource set's compiled classes (`testClassesDirs`) and classpath. `register` is *lazy*: the block\nonly runs if the task is actually needed. (The first version used\n`val loadTest by tasks.registering(Test::class)`, which Gradle 9.7 reports as deprecated.)\n\n**Gradle: `outputs.upToDateWhen { false }`.** Gradle normally skips a task whose inputs haven't\nchanged (\"UP-TO-DATE\"). For a measurement you want a fresh run every time.\n\n**Gradle: passing `-D` through.** `./gradlew loadTest -Dload.seconds=60` sets a property in *Gradle's*\nJVM, but tests run in a separate JVM. `providers.systemProperty(key).orNull` reads it (null if not\ngiven), and `systemProperty(key, it)` forwards it to the test JVM. `?.let { }` only runs when\nit's there, so the test's defaults apply otherwise.\n\n**Kotlin: `tasks.test { }`.** Configures the existing `test` task. The `withType<Test>` block\nabove still applies to both tasks; this adds the tag filter to one of them.\n''')\ns=s.replace(\"- What does `-Xjsr305=strict` change about\",\"- How does `./gradlew loadTest -Dload.seconds=60` reach the test, which runs in a different JVM?\\n- Why does `./gradlew test` not run `InventoryLoadTest`, and what would happen if the `tasks.test { }` block were removed?\\n- What does `-Xjsr305=strict` change about\")\nopen(p,\"w\",encoding=\"utf-8\").write(s)\nEOF",
  "description": "Document the loadTest task in build notes"
}

{

  "content": "# InventoryLoadTest.kt\n\n> Source: [src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/InventoryLoadTest.kt](../../../../../src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/InventoryLoadTest.kt) · Last synced with code: 2026-09-30\n\n## Role\n\nThe **HTTP load test** from the oversell test plan in [TODO.md](../../../../../.claude/TODO.md). The\nrace tests fire one burst of 50 requests. This one keeps 50 clients buying and restocking three\npopular SKUs over real HTTP for 20 seconds, which is tens of thousands of requests. At the end, the\nbooks must balance exactly for every SKU.\n\nIt runs with `./gradlew loadTest` (see [build.gradle.kts](../../build.gradle.kts.md#a-separate-task-for-the-load-test)),\nnot with `./gradlew test`, because it takes a fixed time (decided by Robert, 2026-09-30). It also\nprints throughput and latency, so it doubles as a rough performance check.\n\nA 5-second run on 2026-09-30 (a Windows laptop, Postgres in Docker) gave:\n\n```\nLoad test: 50 clients for 5.0 s\n  requests:   12223 (2439 per second)\n  purchases:  4225 succeeded, 4418 refused\n  restocks:   3580\n  latency ms: p50 18.336, p95 38.576, p99 56.805, max 140.117\n  final stock: {hot-1=8, hot-2=0, hot-3=3}\n```\n\n## Walkthrough\n\n### Setup: a real server on a random port\n\n```kotlin\n@Tag(\"load\")\n@SpringBootTest(\n    webEnvironment = SpringBootTest.WebEnvironment.RANDOM_PORT,\n    // Why: warnings only, so the summary isn't buried under startup logs in the loadTest output.\n    properties = [\"logging.level.root=WARN\"],\n)\n@Import(TestcontainersConfiguration::class)\nclass InventoryLoadTest @Autowired constructor(\n    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,\n    @LocalServerPort private val port: Int,\n) {\n```\n\n**Spring: `WebEnvironment.RANDOM_PORT`.** By default `@SpringBootTest` has no real server (MockMvc\ncalls the controllers directly). `RANDOM_PORT` starts the embedded Tomcat on a free port, so the\ntest talks to it through real sockets with [InventoryHttpClient.kt](../support/InventoryHttpClient.kt.md).\n`@LocalServerPort` injects the port that was picked.\n\n**Spring: `properties = [...]`.** Sets properties for this test's context only. Kotlin writes array\narguments of annotations with `[...]`.\n\n**JUnit: `@Tag(\"load\")`.** The label the Gradle tasks filter on.\n\n### Settings from the command line\n\n```kotlin\nprivate val seconds = System.getProperty(\"load.seconds\")?.toLong() ?: 20\nprivate val clients = System.getProperty(\"load.clients\")?.toInt() ?: 50\n```\n\n**Kotlin: `?.` and `?:` together.** `System.getProperty` returns `String?`. `?.toLong()` converts it\nonly if it's there, and `?: 20` supplies the default otherwise. Java would need\n`p != null ? Long.parseLong(p) : 20`. The build forwards `-Dload.seconds=...` to the test JVM.\n\n### Each client keeps its own tally\n\n```kotlin\n/** What one client did: units sold and received per SKU, outcome counts, and every latency. */\nprivate class Tally {\n    val sold = mutableMapOf<String, Int>()\n    val received = mutableMapOf<String, Int>()\n    var purchased = 0\n    var refused = 0\n    var restocks = 0\n    val latenciesMicros = mutableListOf<Long>()\n}\n```\n\n**Why per client:** each client writes only to its own `Tally`, so the maps and counters need no\nlocks or atomics. The tallies are added up once everyone has finished. The only thing the clients\ncontend on is what's under test: the app and the database.\n\n### The run\n\n```kotlin\nval tallies = Executors.newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor().use { executor ->\n    (0 until clients)\n        .map { i -> executor.submit(Callable { runClient(client, Random(i), deadline) }) }\n        .map { it.get() }\n}\n```\n\n**Java 21: virtual threads.** `newVirtualThreadPerTaskExecutor()` runs each task on a *virtual*\nthread: a lightweight

The oversell guarantee is tested with several different mechanisms: race tests, a negative
control, multi-instance tests, load tests and property-based tests. The full plan is in
[.claude/TODO.md](.claude/TODO.md#oversell-protection-test-plan).

## Build and run

**Prerequisites:** JDK 21 and Docker Desktop (running). Postgres starts automatically in a
container; there is nothing to install or configure by hand.

```sh
./gradlew bootRun   # start the API on http://localhost:8080 (Windows PowerShell: .\gradlew.bat bootRun)
./gradlew test      # run all tests, each against a throwaway Postgres container
./gradlew build     # compile, test and package build/libs/inventory-0.0.1-SNAPSHOT.jar
```

Try it once the app is running:

```sh
curl -i http://localhost:8080/inventory
# HTTP/1.1 200
# Content-Type: application/json
# []

curl -i http://localhost:8080/inventory/CW-XYCS-BM-01
# HTTP/1.1 404
# Content-Type: text/plain
# SKU not found: CW-XYCS-BM-01
```

`bootRun` starts Postgres from [compose.yaml](compose.yaml) automatically (Spring Boot's
Docker Compose support) and stops it on shutdown. Tests don't use Compose; Testcontainers
starts a fresh Postgres for them. No database URL or password needs configuring.

**Troubleshooting (Windows):** if `gradlew` says `JAVA_HOME is not set` right after installing
the JDK, the terminal (or VS Code itself) was opened before the install and has a stale
environment. Close and reopen it completely.

```kotlin
// Why: @SpringBootTest, not @JdbcTest. @JdbcTest's rolled-back transaction belongs to the test thread
// only: rows it inserts stay invisible to the worker threads, and the workers' commits escape the rollback.
/**
 * Oversell test plan, concurrency section: many threads change the same SKU at the same moment
 * through [InventoryRepository], and no update may be lost or fail. ... No race may ever oversell.
 */
@SpringBootTest
@Import(TestcontainersConfiguration::class)
class InventoryConcurrencyTest @Autowired constructor(
    private val inventoryRepository: InventoryRepository,
    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,
**Kotlin: destructuring in lambdas.** `{ (_, result) -> ... }` unpacks the `Pair` into its two
parts right in the parameter list; `_` skips the part the lambda doesn't need. `sumOf { }` adds up
whatever the lambda returns for each element.

### `receive`, `quantityOf`, `inventory`

```kotlin
/** `POST /inventory/{skuId}`: adds stock and returns the new total; fails on anything but 200. */
fun receive(skuId: String, quantity: Int): InventoryItem {
    val response = post("/inventory/$skuId", quantity)
    check(response.statusCode() == 200) { "receive $skuId: ${response.statusCode()} ${response.body()}" }
    return json.readValue(response.body())
}
```

**Kotlin: `check`** (indexed with `require` in the concepts index). `check(condition) { message }` throws `IllegalStateException` when the
condition is false. It's the sibling of `require` (`IllegalArgumentException`, "you called me
wrong"): `check` means "the state I found is wrong", here an unexpected response.

`quantityOf` returns `Int?` (`null` on a 404), mirroring the `quantityOf` helpers that the
repository-level tests run against the database.

### The request helpers
````

<details><summary>Tool call: Bash — Add quantityOf and inventory coverage to HTTP notes</summary>

````json
{
  "command": "cd \"C:/Users/rober/Documents/Projects/Nuuly_Inventory_Assessment\" && python - <<'EOF'\ndef edit(p, pairs):\n    s=open(p,encoding=\"utf-8\").read()\n    for a,b in pairs:\n        assert a in s, (p,a[:80]); s=s.replace(a,b,1)\n    open(p,\"w\",encoding=\"utf-8\").write(s)\nedit(\"docs/learning/notes/test/support/InventoryHttpClient.kt.md\",[\n('''`quantityOf` returns `Int?` (`null` on a 404), mirroring the `quantityOf` helpers that the\nrepository-level tests run against the database.\n''','''```kotlin\n/** `GET /inventory/{skuId}`: the SKU's stock, or `null` on a 404. */\nfun quantityOf(skuId: String): Int? {\n    val response = http.send(request(\"/inventory/$skuId\").GET().build(), HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString())\n    return when (response.statusCode()) {\n        200 -> json.readValue<InventoryItem>(response.body()).quantity\n        404 -> null\n        else -> error(\"get $skuId: ${response.statusCode()} ${response.body()}\")\n    }\n}\n\n/** `GET /inventory`: every SKU, in the order the app returns them. */\nfun inventory(): List<InventoryItem> {\n    val response = http.send(request(\"/inventory\").GET().build(), HttpResponse.BodyHandlers.ofString())\n    check(response.statusCode() == 200) { \"list: ${response.statusCode()} ${response.body()}\" }\n    return json.readValue(response.body())\n}\n```\n\n**Kotlin: `Int?` as \"maybe absent\".** `quantityOf` returns `null` for a 404, mirroring the\n`quantityOf` helpers the repository-level tests run against the database, so callers write\n`assertEquals(0, client.quantityOf(\"widget\"))` the same way.\n\n**Kotlin: `when` with a subject.** `when (response.statusCode()) { 200 -> ...; 404 -> ... }` compares\nthe value against each branch, like Java's `switch`. Here the status alone decides, unlike in\n`purchase`, where a 400 also needs its body checked.\n\n**Jackson: reading a list.** In `inventory()`, the return type `List<InventoryItem>` tells\n`readValue` what to build, generic element type included. That works because `readValue` is\nreified: the full type survives to runtime, which Java's `readValue(json, List.class)` can't do.\n'''),\n])\nEOF",
  "description": "Add quantityOf and inventory coverage to HTTP notes"
}
````

{

The race helper in\n[Race.kt](../support/Race.kt.md) uses a fixed pool of platform threads instead, because it needs\nexactly N threads waiting at a gate.''','''(waiting on HTTP) costs almost nothing, so thousands are fine. The race helper in\n[Race.kt](../support/Race.kt.md) uses a fixed pool of ordinary *platform* threads. Virtual threads\nwould work there too (a virtual thread waiting on a `CountDownLatch` is simply parked); `race`\njust predates this test and 50 platform threads are cheap enough. One real difference to know: on\nJava 21, a virtual thread that blocks *inside a `synchronized` block* is \"pinned\" to its carrier\nthread and loses its advantage (fixed in Java 24).\n\n**Java: `.map { it.get() }`.** `Future.get()` waits for a client to finish and returns its `Tally`. If\nthe client threw (an unexpected HTTP answer, a timeout), `get()` rethrows it wrapped in an\n`ExecutionException`, which fails the test with the original cause attached.'''),\n('''- Why can this test use virtual threads, while `race` uses a fixed pool of platform threads?''',\n '''- What makes virtual threads a good fit for this test? Would `race` also work on virtual threads?'''),\n('''### Each client keeps its own tally''','''### Seeding and the clock\n\n```kotlin\n// Why: demand (70% of calls, 1-3 units) outruns supply (30%, 1-3 units), so every SKU spends\n// most of the run near zero, where an oversell would happen, instead of far from it.\n...\nfun `sustained load on hot SKUs never oversells and keeps an exact balance`() {\n    jdbcClient.sql(\"DELETE FROM inventory\").update()\n    val client = InventoryHttpClient(\"http://localhost:$port\")\n    hotSkus.forEach { client.receive(it, initialStock) }\n    val deadline = System.nanoTime() + TimeUnit.SECONDS.toNanos(seconds)\n\n    val started = System.nanoTime()\n```\n\n**Why seed over HTTP:** the three SKUs start at 100 units each through `POST /inventory/{skuId}`,\nthe same path a real client would use. The `DELETE` goes straight to the database: no endpoint\ndeletes, and the table must start empty because the Spring context (and its Postgres) may be\nshared with other tests.\n\n**Java: `System.nanoTime()`.** A clock for measuring *durations*, in nanoseconds. Unlike\n`currentTimeMillis()`, it never jumps when the system clock is adjusted, so it's the right tool for\ndeadlines and latencies. `TimeUnit.SECONDS.toNanos(seconds)` converts the run length.\n`(System.nanoTime() - started) / 1e9` turns the elapsed time into seconds: `1e9` is a `Double`\nliteral (10⁹), so the division gives a `Double`, not a truncated `Long`.\n\n### Each client keeps its own tally'''),\n])\nEOF",
  "description": "Fix load test notes and add seeding section"
}

It's\nused only for the `@BeforeEach` cleanup (`DELETE FROM inventory`); since both apps share the\ndatabase, emptying it through A empties it for B too. We'll go focused, starting with the quick tour.

## 1. build.gradle.kts (quick tour)

**Role:** two new test libraries, and a second test task so the slow load test stays out of normal builds.

```kotlin
testImplementation("com.atlassian.oai:openapi-request-validator-mockmvc:3.0.0")
testImplementation("io.kotest:kotest-property:6.2.5")
```

- **Versions are written out** because neither library is in Spring Boot's BOM, the list that supplies versions for everything else.
- **`testImplementation`** means they're only on the test classpath and never ship in the jar.
- **Only `kotest-property`**, not Kotest's own test runner, so the property test stays an ordinary JUnit `@Test`.

```kotlin
tasks.test {
    useJUnitPlatform { excludeTags("load") }
}

tasks.register<Test>("loadTest") {
    ...
    useJUnitPlatform { includeTags("load") }
    outputs.upToDateWhen { false }
    ...
}
```

- `@Tag("load")` on `InventoryLoadTest` is a label. The normal `test` task **excludes** that label, and the new `loadTest` task **includes only** it.
- `tasks.register<Test>` creates a second task of the same type as `test`. `register` is lazy: Gradle only configures it if you actually run it.
- `outputs.upToDateWhen { false }`: Gradle normally skips a task whose inputs haven't changed ("UP-TO-DATE"). A load test is a measurement, so it should run fresh every time.
- The `-Dload.seconds=60` flag is set in *Gradle's* JVM. The task forwards it to the separate JVM the tests run in.

**Check question:** you run `./gradlew build`. Does the load test run, and which line decides that? Then you run `./gradlew loadTest` twice in a row without changing any code. What would happen the second time *without* the `upToDateWhen { false }` line?

Both are right. Two refinements:

- **"Old data" more precisely:** Gradle wouldn't run the tests at all. It would print `loadTest UP-TO-DATE` and reuse the previous pass/fail result, so no new requests go out and no summary is printed. For a measurement, that means you learn nothing new.

## 2. support/Race.kt (quick tour)

**Role:** the start gate you've seen before. It moved out of `InventoryConcurrencyTest` into `support/` so four test classes can share it.

```kotlin
fun <T> race(threads: Int, action: (Int) -> T): List<Result<T>> {
```

What's new here is only **where** it lives: a **top-level function**, not inside a class. Other files `import com.robertwoolbright.inventory.support.race` and call it like any function. Java has no free-standing functions, so the compiler puts it in a generated class `RaceKt`, and Java would call `RaceKt.race(...)`. It's like a `public static` method on a utility class.

The inside is unchanged: two `CountDownLatch`es hold every thread at the gate and release them together, and `runCatching` collects each thread's result or exception without stopping the race.

**Check question:** `race` returns `List<Result<T>>`, not a plain `List<T>`. What would we lose if it threw on the first failed thread instead?

## User — 2026-09-30T17:42:27.919Z

The info from the exception. We want the full response not just a single value from a response.

### Claude

Half right. We *would* still get the exception, since throwing carries it along. What we'd lose is **everyone else's result**.

Think of 50 buyers where 30 fail. If `race` threw on the first failure:

- the test report shows **one** exception, and you can't tell whether 1 or 30 buyers failed, or whether they all failed the same way;
- the 20 successful results are thrown away, so you can't run any checks on them.

With `List<Result<T>>`, every thread's outcome comes back, success or failure. The test decides what to do with them all: `"purchases failed: $failures"` prints *all* the failures at once.

Now we slow down for the three focus files.

## 3. OversellAssertions.kt (full depth)

**Role:** the oversell rule, written once as a function. The real race test expects it to **pass**; the negative control expects it to **fail**.

**Chunk 1: why it's shared** ([OversellAssertions.kt:7-8](src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/OversellAssertions.kt#L7-L8))

```kotlin
// Why: one shared check, so the negative control proves that the exact assertions guarding the
// real repository can detect an oversell, not a separate check written to fail.
```

This is the whole idea behind a negative control. Suppose the negative control had its **own** assertions. It would only prove that *those* can fail. The real test could still have a weak check, such as only `final >= 0`, which your naive code passes, since it ends at 9. One shared function removes that gap: the check that guards the real code is the same one that's proven to catch the bug.

**Chunk 2: `require` vs. `assert`** ([OversellAssertions.kt:16-18](src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/OversellAssertions.kt#L16-L18))

```kotlin
fun assertNoOversell(stock: Int, outcomes: List<PurchaseResult>, finalQuantity: Int?) {
    require(outcomes.size >= stock) { "needs at least $stock buyers, got ${outcomes.size}" }
    assertTrue(outcomes.none { it == PurchaseResult.SkuNotFound }, "an existing SKU was reported missing")
```

There are two kinds of failure here:

- **`require`** throws `IllegalArgumentException`: *the test itself* is set up wrong. With 5 buyers for 10 units, you can't expect "exactly 10 sold", so it refuses to judge.
- **`assertTrue` / `assertEquals`** throw `AssertionError`: *the code under test* is wrong.

This matters because the negative control expects an `AssertionError`. If someone misconfigured it with too few buyers, `require` throws the *other* type, and the negative control fails instead of passing by accident.

`none { }` is true when no element matches, the opposite of `any { }`.

**Chunk 3: four checks, four different bugs** ([OversellAssertions.kt:20-24](src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/OversellAssertions.kt#L20-L24))

```kotlin
val remaining = outcomes.filterIsInstance<PurchaseResult.Purchased>().map { it.item.quantity }.sorted()
assertTrue(remaining.size <= stock, "oversold: ${remaining.size} purchases succeeded for a stock of $stock")
assertEquals((0 until stock).toList(), remaining, "each unit must be sold exactly once")
assertEquals(outcomes.size - stock, outcomes.count { it == PurchaseResult.InsufficientStock }, "refused purchases")
assertEquals(0, finalQuantity, "final quantity")
```

`remaining` is the "units left" number each *successful* buyer was told, sorted. `filterIsInstance<Purchased>()` keeps only the successes **and** types the list as `List<Purchased>`, so `it.item` compiles without a cast. It's a smart cast for a whole list.

With stock 10 and 50 buyers, a correct run gives:

| Check | Correct run | Bug it catches |
|---|---|---|
| `remaining.size <= stock` | 10 ≤ 10 | **Oversell:** more sales than units |
| `remaining == [0..9]` | each buyer saw a different number | **Lost update:** two buyers told "9 left" |
| refused = 50 − 10 | 40 refused | a buyer wrongly refused, or given the wrong error |
| final = 0 | 0 | stock left over, or negative |

**Check question:** your naive repository with 12 buyers returns twelve `Purchased(widget, 9)`, and the database ends at 9. Walk through the four checks. Which ones fail, and which one fails **first**?

### Claude

You spotted the oversell. The mix-up is in what "pass" means for an assertion. An assertion states what **must be true**. When the real value breaks it, the assertion **fails**, and that's the test catching the bug.

Your naive run gives twelve `Purchased(widget, 9)`, and the database ends at 9:

| # | Assertion says | Actual | Result |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | successes ≤ 10 | 12 | **fails:** "oversold: 12 purchases succeeded for a stock of 10" |
| 2 | remaining = [0, 1, …, 9] | [9, 9, …, 9] | fails |
| 3 | refused = 12 − 10 = **2** | **0**, everyone "succeeded" | fails |
| 4 | final = 0 | 9 | fails |

On 3: no exception is involved. It only counts `InsufficientStock` results. The correct code would refuse the 2 extra buyers, but the naive code refused nobody, so 0 ≠ 2.

**All four would fail, but only #1 is reported.** An assertion throws as soon as it fails, so the function stops there and the rest never run. That's why #1 comes first: it's the invariant itself, and its message names the actual bug. `NegativeControlTest` checks that the message starts with `oversold`, to prove it's caught for the right reason. The "SKU exists" check before it passes, because the SKU does exist.

**Quick re-check:** suppose I moved check 4 (`final == 0`) to the top of the function. What message would the negative control see instead, and why would its `startsWith("oversold")` assertion then fail?

## User — 2026-09-30T17:51:28.866Z

Well start with oversold assertion would fail because you are moving the 4th assertion to the top. I think it would still fail because it still would end at 9.

### Claude

Right: the final-quantity check would fail first, because it's still 9. The last piece is what that does to the negative control.

- `assertNoOversell` now throws at the **new first line**, with the message `final quantity ==> expected: <0> but was: <9>`.
- The negative control catches that `AssertionError` fine. But its next line checks `caught.message.startsWith("oversold")`, and this message starts with `final quantity`. So **the negative control test fails**, even though the bug *was* detected.

That's deliberate strictness. The negative control doesn't just ask "did something fail?" It asks "did it fail **because of an oversell**?" A check that fails for some unrelated reason proves nothing about oversell detection. It's the same lesson as the contract test's exact-key self-checks.

// Why: This repository lives only in test because the class is deliberately unsafe.
// We have it so NegativeControlTest can prove the oversell check catches a lost update.
/**
 * A purchase done the naive way: read the stock, check it in Kotlin, then write an absolute value.
 * Correct when buyers take turns; under concurrency, buyers overwrite each other and it oversells.
 */
class NaiveInventoryRepository(
    private val jdbcClient: JdbcClient,
    private val pauseBeforeWrite: () -> Unit
) {
```

4. There's no `@Repository` on it. How does the test get an instance?

### Claude

**1. Correct.** One more detail: code under `src/test` is never packaged into the jar, so it can't ship by accident.

**2. Half there.** `Unit` is the "void" part: it returns nothing useful. But read the whole type:

- `()`: it takes **no arguments**
- `->`: "returns"
- `Unit`: nothing useful

So `() -> Unit` isn't a value you compute. It's a **function** you're holding. `pauseBeforeWrite` stores *a piece of code*, and `pauseBeforeWrite()` runs it. The test decides what that code is: `{}` does nothing, and `{ barrier.await(...) }` waits for the other buyers.

**3. This one's a mix-up.** `private val` has nothing to do with the return type. It controls whether the constructor argument is **kept** after the object is built. The two words do separate jobs:

| Keyword | Job |
|---|---|
| `val` | **Store** it as a property, so `removeStock` can use it later |
| `private` | Only code inside this class can see that property |

Without `val`, `pauseBeforeWrite` is only a constructor argument. It exists while the object is being built and is gone afterwards. Then the `pauseBeforeWrite()` call on line 32 wouldn't compile: "unresolved reference". It's the same reason `jdbcClient` has `val`: `removeStock` uses both long after construction. In Java terms, `private val x` generates the `private final` field plus the `this.x = x` assignment.

**4. Correct.** The test builds it by hand: `NaiveInventoryRepository(jdbcClient) { ... }`. It passes in the `JdbcClient` bean Spring injected into the test, and the lambda goes after the parentheses because it's the last parameter.

**Chunk 2: `removeStock`** ([NaiveInventoryRepository.kt:17-40](src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/NaiveInventoryRepository.kt#L17-L40)). ### Claude

That's the right walk-through, and six steps is just how I counted them. Two refinements on the gap:

**1. The gap isn't only the pause.** It's everything between the `SELECT` and the `UPDATE`: the Kotlin check, the subtraction, and the pause. Delete `pauseBeforeWrite()` and the gap still exists in production; it's just microseconds wide instead of held open. The pause only makes the bad timing happen on every run instead of occasionally.

**2. "Out of date" understates it. The write itself is destructive.** Picture buyers A and B:

| | Buyer A | Buyer B |
|---|---|---|
| read | 10 | 10 |
| compute | 9 | 9 |
| write | `SET quantity = 9` | `SET quantity = 9` (**erases A's sale**) |

It's not only that A's returned number is stale. B's `SET quantity = 9` **overwrites** A's write, because it's an absolute value computed from an old read. That's the lost update: two units sold, the database says one. And since 9 is a valid number, `CHECK (quantity >= 0)` never objects.

The real `removeStock` closes the gap by putting the check and the write in **one statement**: `UPDATE ... SET quantity = quantity - :q WHERE quantity >= :q`. Postgres locks the row, so B waits and then re-checks against A's result.

## 5. NegativeControlTest.kt

**Role:** your class run twice, through the same race and the same check.

**Chunk 1: control 1, buyers take turns** ([NegativeControlTest.kt:45-52](src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/NegativeControlTest.kt#L45-L52))

```kotlin
@Test
fun `the naive purchase passes the oversell check when buyers take turns`() {
    val naive = NaiveInventoryRepository(jdbcClient) {}

    val outcomes = (1..buyers).map { naive.removeStock("widget", 1) }

    assertNoOversell(stock, outcomes, quantityOf("widget"))
}
```

This calls your code 12 times in a row on the test thread, with `{}` as the pause. It sells 10, refuses 2, and ends at 0, so the check **passes**.

**Chunk 2: control 2, buyers race** ([NegativeControlTest.kt:58-74](src/test/kotlin/com/robertwoolbright/inventory/inventory/NegativeControlTest.kt#L58-L74))

```kotlin
val barrier = CyclicBarrier(buyers)
val naive = NaiveInventoryRepository(jdbcClient) { barrier.await(10, TimeUnit.SECONDS) }

val results = race(buyers) { naive.removeStock("widget", 1) }
...
val caught = assertFailsWith<AssertionError> {
    assertNoOversell(stock, outcomes, quantityOf("widget"))
}
assertTrue(caught.message.orEmpty().startsWith("oversold"), ...)
```

- **`CyclicBarrier(12)`** is a meeting point. Each buyer calls `await()` inside your pause, *after its read*, and blocks until all 12 have arrived. Only then do they all continue to the write. So every buyer reads 10 before anyone writes 9, on every run: a guaranteed worst case.
- **Compare `race`'s `CountDownLatch`:** the latch lines threads up at the *start*. The barrier holds them in the *middle*, between read and write. The start gate makes them overlap; the barrier makes them overlap at exactly the wrong moment.
- **`assertFailsWith<AssertionError> { }`** passes only if the block throws that type, and hands the exception back so we can check its message. It's JUnit's `assertThrows` in Java.

**Check question:** what would it mean if **control 1** failed? And why is the test more convincing *with* control 1 than without it?
